and functions
h_{1},h_{2}\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}]
such that,
f_{i}|_{A}=h_{i}|_{A}
for each
i\in[k]
.

Proof.

The proof is deferred to
Appendix

C
.
∎

Similarly, for the individual degree case our result reads as follows:

Theorem 4.3

(Correlated Individual-
\RM
Proximity Generator)
.

Let
\varepsilon\geq\frac{20}{q^{2/15}}
and suppose
f_{1},f_{2}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfy

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}\left[{\sf agr}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{i}\cdot f_{i},\RM_{q^{\prime}}\left[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\right]\right)\geq\varepsilon\right]\geq 2\cdot{\sf err}(2d,q,q^{\prime}),

then there is a subset of points
A\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
of size

\mu(A)\geq 0.99\varepsilon

and oracle functions
h_{1},h_{2}\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}]
such that,
f_{i}|_{A}=h_{i}|_{A}
for each
i\in[2]
.

Proof.

The proof is deferred to
Section

C.3
.
∎

4.2.2
Proximity Generators without Correlated Agreement

We also need versions of
Theorem

4.1
for larger
k
’s. In that case, our argument gets a conclusion weaker than the one in
Theorem

4.1
. Instead of being able to say that there is a sizable set
A
wherein all
f_{i}
’s agree with some low-degree function, we are only able to guarantee that each
f_{i}
has non-trivial agreement with a degree
d
polynomial (i.e., the points of agreement of
f_{i}
with its low-degree polynomial may be completely different than those of
f_{i^{\prime}}
). This statement is sufficient for our purposes, but we suspect it should
be possible to get the stronger conclusion.

Theorem 4.4

(Uncorrelated
\RM
Proximity Generator)
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be oracle functions. If

\Pr_{(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\sim{\sf prox}(k,d,q,q^{\prime})}\left[{\sf agr}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{i}\cdot f_{i},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}]\right)\geq\varepsilon\right]\geq 2k\cdot{\sf err}(d,q,q^{\prime}),

then

{\sf agr}\left(f_{i},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}]\right)\geq 0.99\varepsilon\quad\forall i\in[k].

Proof.

The proof is deferred to
Appendix

C
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

We remark that our results only hold for agreement parameter
\varepsilon\geq\Omega\left((d/q)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}\right)
, whereas the univariate Reed-Solomon proximity generator holds for
\varepsilon
as small as the Johnson bound,
\varepsilon=\sqrt{d/q}
. The FRI conjecture posits that the univariate Reed-Solomon proximity generator holds for the smallest possible agreement,
\varepsilon=d/q
. It is an intriguing future direction to improve the quantitative results of our proximity generators. One avenue towards this would be to improve
\tau_{{\sf line}}
in the soundness of the line versus point tests. We remark that higher dimensional versions of the line versus point test (which use planes or cubes) achieve improved and even optimal dependence on
q
in the soundness
[
MR08
,
BDN17
,
MZ23
]
.

4.3
Combining Functions of Different Degrees

Next, we give versions of proximity generators in the case the degrees of the various functions may be different.

4.3.1
Univariate

We first show how to go from a univariate function far from degree
d
to a univariate function far from degree
d^{\prime}\geq d
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be functions, let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell}
be degree parameters and let
d^{\prime}
be a target degree which is greater than all of the
d_{i}
. Then the combined function is generated as follows:

•

Choose
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
according to the Reed-Solomon proximity generator, and choose
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random.

•

Set

{\sf combine}_{d^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\xi_{i}\cdot(f_{i}+\xi_{0}\cdot x^{d^{\prime}-d_{i}}\cdot f_{i}).

Clearly, if
f_{i}
has degree at most
d_{i}
,
then
f_{i}+\xi_{0}\cdot x^{d^{\prime}-d_{i}}\cdot f_{i}
has degree at
most
d^{\prime}
. The following lemma shows that the converse direct also works in a robust sense:

Lemma 4.7
.

Fix degree parameters
d\leq d^{\prime}
and an agreement parameter
\varepsilon\geq\sqrt{\frac{d^{\prime}}{q}}
. Suppose
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfies
{\sf agr}(f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
. Then

\Pr_{\xi\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[{\sf agr}(f+\xi\cdot x^{d^{\prime}-d}\cdot f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d^{\prime},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq\varepsilon]<{\sf err}=\frac{{\sf poly}(d^{\prime})}{q^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this is not the case. Then, by
Theorem

4.1
, there exists
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
of fractional size at least
\varepsilon
and degree
d
functions
g_{1},g_{2}
such that such that
f|_{U}=g_{1}|_{U}
and
x^{d^{\prime}-d}\cdot f|_{U}=g_{2}|_{U}
. It follows that

x^{d^{\prime}-d}\cdot g_{1}|_{U}=g_{2}|_{U}.

Since
U
has fractional size greater than
d^{\prime}/q
, it follows by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma that
x^{d^{\prime}-d}\cdot g_{1}=g_{2}
. Hence
g_{1}
has degree at most
d
and agrees with
f
on a set of fractional size greater than
\varepsilon
, which is a contradiction.
∎

We now give our proximity generators in the case the functions have different degrees.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be functions and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell}
be agreement parameters. Let
d^{\prime}\geq\max_{i}(d_{i})
and suppose
i
,
{\sf agr}(f_{i},\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d_{i},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon
satisfying

\varepsilon\geq\sqrt{\frac{d^{\prime}}{q}}.

Then,

\Pr[{\sf agr}({\sf combine}_{d^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}),\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d^{\prime},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq\varepsilon]\leq\ell\cdot{\sf err},

where the probability is over the randomness in the combine function.

Proof.

Let
i
be the index such that
{\sf agr}(f_{i},\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d_{i},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
. Then,

\Pr_{\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[{\sf agr}(f_{i}+\xi_{0}\cdot x^{d^{\prime}-d_{i}}\cdot f_{i},\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d^{\prime},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq\varepsilon]\leq{\sf err}.

by
Lemma

4.7
. Conditioned on this not being the case, we have that,

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[{\sf agr}({\sf combine}_{d^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}),\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d^{\prime},\mathbb{F}_{q}]\geq\varepsilon]\leq(\ell-1)\cdot{\sf err},

by
Theorem

4.1
. The lemma follows from a union bound.
∎

4.3.2
Multivariate

We now give a multi-variate version of our proximity generators for functions with different degrees, closely following the ideas in the univariate case.

Individual Degree:

First show how to combine degrees while preserving proximity to the individual degree Reed-Muller code. In this case,
the “combine” operation works as follows:

Definition 4.9
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be oracle functions, let
(d_{i,1},d_{i,2})
be degree parameters for
i\in[\ell]
, and let
(d^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be a target degree which is greater than all of the
(d_{i,1},d_{i,2})
. Then the combined function is generated as follows:

•

Choose
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
according to the Reed-Solomon proximity generator, and choose
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random.

•

Set

{\sf combine}_{(d^{\prime},d^{\prime})}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\xi_{i}\cdot(f_{i}+\xi_{0}\cdot x_{1}^{d^{\prime}-d_{i,1}}x_{2}^{d^{\prime}-d_{i,2}}\cdot f_{i}).

Lemma 4.10
.

Fix degree parameters
(d_{1},d_{2})\leq(d^{\prime},d^{\prime})
and
\varepsilon\geq 20\cdot\left(\frac{d^{\prime}}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
.
Suppose that
f:\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfies
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
. Then

\Pr_{\xi\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[{\sf agr}(f+\xi\cdot x_{1}^{d^{\prime}-d_{1}}x_{2}^{d^{\prime}-d_{2}}\cdot f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq 1.01\varepsilon]<{\sf err}=\frac{{\sf poly}(d^{\prime})}{q^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this is not the case. Then, by
Theorem

4.3
, there exists
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}
of fractional size at least
0.99\varepsilon
and degree
(d,d)
functions
g_{1},g_{2}
such that such that

f|_{U}=g_{1}|_{U}\quad\text{and}\quad x_{1}^{d^{\prime}-d_{1}}x_{2}^{d^{\prime}-d_{2}}\cdot f|_{U}=g_{2}|_{U}.

It follows that

x_{1}^{d^{\prime}-d_{1}}x_{2}^{d^{\prime}-d_{2}}\cdot g_{1}|_{U}=g_{2}|_{U}.

Since
U
has fractional size greater than
d^{\prime}/q
, it follows that
x_{1}^{d^{\prime}-d_{1}}x_{2}^{d^{\prime}-d_{2}}\cdot g_{1}=g_{2}
by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma. Hence
g_{1}
must have individual degrees at most
(d_{1},d_{2})
and agree with
f
on a set of fractional size greater than
0.99\varepsilon
. This is a contradiction.
∎

The proximity generator in this now easily follows:

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}:\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be functions, let
(d_{i,1},d_{i,2})
be individual degree parameters for
i\in[\ell]
, let
(d^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be a target degree which is greater than each
(d_{i,1},d_{i,2})
, and let
\varepsilon\geq 21\left(\frac{d^{\prime}}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
be an agreement parameter. Suppose that
{\sf agr}(f_{i},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d_{i,1},d_{i,2}),\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
for some
i\in[\ell]
. Then

\Pr[{\sf agr}({\sf combine}_{(d^{\prime},d^{\prime})}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}),\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\mathbb{F}_{q}]\geq 1.03\varepsilon]\leq\ell\cdot{\sf err},

where the probability is over the randomness in the
{\sf combine}
function.

Proof.

Let
i
be an index such that
{\sf agr}(f_{i},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d_{i,1},d_{i,2}),\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
. Then,

\Pr_{\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[{\sf agr}(f_{i}+\xi_{0}\cdot x_{1}^{d^{\prime}-d_{i,1}}x_{2}^{d^{\prime}-d_{i,2}}\cdot f_{i},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq 1.01\varepsilon]\leq{\sf err}

by
Lemma

4.10
. Conditioned on this not being the case, we have that,

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}\left[{\sf agr}\left({\sf combine}_{(d^{\prime},d^{\prime})}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}),\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\mathbb{F}_{q}]\right)\geq 1.03\varepsilon\right]\leq(\ell-1){\sf err},

by the contrapositive of
Theorem

4.3
. The lemma follows from a union bound.
∎

Total Degree:

For the total degree Reed-Muller code, we can show the following analogues of
Lemma

4.11
. The proofs are nearly identical to that of
Lemma

4.11
so we omit them. The only difference is that we apply the Schwartz-Zippel lemma to the total degree Reed-Muller code in the analogue of
Lemma

4.10
and we appeal to the total degree Reed-Muller proximity generator theorem (
Theorem

4.4
) instead of the individual degree version (
Theorem

4.3
). We state our results on the total degree Reed-Muller for arbitrary dimension
m
, but for our purposes
m
will always be
2
or
3
.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}:\mathbb{F}^{m}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be oracle functions, let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell}
be degree parameters and let
d^{\prime}
be a target degree which is greater than all of the
d_{i}
. Then the combined function is generated as follows:

•

Choose
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
according to the total degree Reed-Muller proximity generator, and choose
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random.

•

Set

{\sf combine}_{d^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\xi_{i}\cdot(f_{i}+\xi_{0}\cdot x_{1}^{d^{\prime}-d_{i}}\cdot f_{i}).

Here we reuse the
{\sf combine}
notation from the univariate case in
Definition

4.6
, but note that it will be clear from context which case we are referring to based on if the input functions are univariate or multivariate.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}:\mathbb{F}^{m}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be functions, let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell}
be degree parameters for
i\in[\ell]
, let
d^{\prime}\geq\max_{i}(d_{i})
be a target degree and let
\varepsilon\geq 20\cdot 1.01^{m}\left(\frac{d^{\prime}}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
be an agreement parameter. Suppose that
{\sf agr}(f_{i},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d_{i},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
for some
i\in[\ell]
. Then

\Pr[{\sf agr}({\sf combine}_{d^{\prime}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}),\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq 1.01^{m}\varepsilon]\leq\ell\cdot{\sf err},

where the probability is over the randomness in the
{\sf combine}
function.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
Lemma

4.11
.
∎

5
Quotienting to Remove Side Conditions

We will often be interested in testing the proximity of some
f:U\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
to a family of functions, say
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
, subject to some constraints which we call side conditions. More specifically, we will want to test the proximity of
f
to the subfamily of
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
consisting of
g
’s satisfying that
g|_{A}=h|_{A}
for some prespecified
A
and
h:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. We refer to
h
as the side condition function,
A
as the set of side condition points, and use the notation

\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]=\{g\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}]\;|\;g|_{A}=h|_{A}\}.

We define
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
and
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
analogously.

The goal of this section is to we present a technique called quotienting, which will reduce the proximity testing with side conditions problem to standard proximity testing without side conditions. We will start by discussing quotienting in the univariate case, which was first given in
[
ACY23
,
ACFY24a
]
, and then move on to quotienting in the multivariate case.

5.1
Univariate Quotienting

Fix an
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and a side condition function
h:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. We remark that we do not require
A\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
; in that case, one should interpret the side conditions from
h
as being conditions on the low degree extension of
f
to
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. That is, we are expecting that the low degree extension of
f
over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
,
\widehat{f}
, satisfies
\widehat{f}|_{A}=h|_{A}
. Suppose we want to test proximity of
f
to the family
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
, where the size of
A
should be thought of as small compared to
d
.
How should we go about doing this?

Let
\widehat{f}
and
\widehat{h}
be the low-degree extensions of
f
and
h
respectively from
\mathbb{F}_{q}
to
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. Also let
\tilde{h}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be denoted by
\widehat{h}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}
. The idea behind quotienting is the following fact: if
f\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
, then by
Lemma

3.14
we may write
f
as:

\widehat{f}(x)=\widehat{V}_{A}\cdot\widehat{g}(x)+\widehat{h}(x),

(4)

where, recall,
\widehat{V}_{A}(x)=\prod_{\alpha\in A}(x-a)
is the vanishing polynomial over
A
. We note that the polynomial
\widehat{g}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d-|A|}
is uniquely determined, and given access to
f
, the verifier can simulate access to
\widehat{g}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}\setminus A
. Thus, to test proximity of
f
to
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
, the verifier can use this simulated access to
\widehat{g}
and instead attempt to test proximity of
\widehat{g}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}
to
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d-|A|,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
. There is a minor issue with this idea, since the verifier cannot access the values of
\widehat{g}
over
A
. To remedy this, the prover also fills in the values of
\widehat{g}
on
A
. Since
|A|
is small, a cheating prover will not be able to use this to their advantage and affect the acceptance probability of the verifier by much. In terms of completeness, an honest prover can simply provide the true values and this way not decrease the acceptance probability of the verifier. Our discussion motivates the following definition of a quotient function:

Definition 5.1
.

Given a function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, and a fill function
{\sf Fill}:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, define the following function from
\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
,

{\sf Quo}_{1}(f,A,{\sf Fill})(x),=\begin{cases}\frac{f(x)}{V_{A}(x)}\quad\text{if }x\notin A\\
{\sf Fill}(x)\quad\text{if }x\in A.\end{cases}

(5)

The next lemma shows that the honest prover can indeed provide values for
{\sf Fill}
which makes the quotient above a low degree function.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
f\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
, then there is some
{\sf Fill}:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
for which
{\sf Quo}_{1}(f-\tilde{h},A,{\sf Fill})\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d-|A|,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
.

Proof.

Consider the function

g(x)=\frac{\widehat{f}(x)-\widehat{h}(x)}{\widehat{V}_{A}(x)}.

Since the numerator vanishes on
A
, it follows that
\widehat{V}_{A}
divides it, and so
g
is a polynomial. Considering degrees, it follows that
g\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d-|A|}[x]
. Taking
{\sf Fill}
to be the evaluation over
A
of
g
, we get that
{\sf Quo}_{1}(f-\tilde{h},A,{\sf Fill})
is the evaluation of
g
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
∎

On the other hand, the following lemma shows that if
f
is far from
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
, then for any
{\sf Fill}:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, the quotiented function
{\sf Quo}_{1}(f-\tilde{h},A,{\sf Fill})
is far from
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d-|A|,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
.

Lemma 5.3
.

If
{\sf agr}(f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h])\leq\varepsilon
, then for any
{\sf Fill}:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, we have

{\sf agr}({\sf Quo}_{1}(f-\tilde{h},A,{\sf Fill}),\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d-|A|,\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon+\frac{|A|}{q}.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
{\sf Fill}:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and let
g={\sf Quo}_{1}(f-\tilde{h},A,{\sf Fill})
. Suppose
g|_{U}=F|_{U}
for some
F\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d-|A|,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
. Then
F\cdot\widehat{V}_{A}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}+\tilde{h}\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;h]
, and for
x\in U\setminus A
, we have

F(x)\cdot\widehat{V}_{A}(x)+\widehat{h}(x)=g(x)\cdot\widehat{V}_{A}(x)+\widehat{h}(x)=f(x).

It follows that
F\cdot\widehat{V}_{A}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}+\tilde{h}
agrees with
f
on
U\setminus A
. Thus,
\mu(U\setminus A)\leq\varepsilon
and
\mu(U)\leq\varepsilon+|A|/q
.
∎

5.2
Bivariate Quotienting

Bivariate Quotienting is a natural extension of univariate quotienting. Fix a function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and consider a side condition function
h:A\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. Note that here we require the side condition function to be defined over a subcube. Similarly to the univariate case, we do not require
A\times B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
, and we think of
A,B
as small compared to
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Throughout this section, let
\widehat{V}_{A},\widehat{V}_{B}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x]
be the vanishing polynomials of
A
and
B
and
V_{A}
and
V_{B}
be their evaluations over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Also let
\widehat{h}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{(|A|-1,|B|-1)}[x,y]
be the low degree extension of
h
, and let
\tilde{h}
be
\widehat{h}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}
.

Suppose we want to test proximity of
f
to
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
. By
Lemma

3.16
,
we may write the low degree extension of
f\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
as:

\widehat{f}(x,y)=\widehat{V}_{A}(x)\cdot\widehat{g}_{1}(x,y)+\widehat{V}_{B}(y)\cdot\widehat{g}_{2}(x,y)+\widehat{h}(x,y),

(6)

where
\widehat{g}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}^{(d-|A|,d)}[x,y],\widehat{g}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{(d,d-|B|)}[x,y]
. We note that (
6
) suggests that in order to test proximity of
f
to
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
, the verifier can instead attempt to simultaneously test the proximities of
\widehat{g}_{1}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}
to
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d-|A|,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}]
and
\widehat{g}_{2}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}
to
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d-|B|),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}]
.
3
3

3

Ultimately, we will use proximity generators in a suitable fashion to reduce this to testing proximity to a single family, without side conditions.

One difference with the univariate case is that the verifier cannot access
\widehat{g}_{1}
or
\widehat{g}_{2}
given only access to
\widehat{f}
and
\widehat{h}
. To resolve this, the prover will provide
g_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, in which scenario the verifier will be able to simulate access to
\widehat{g}_{2}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\setminus(\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B)
. Since
B
is small, this is nearly all of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
, and as in the univariate case the prover fills in the rest of the values of
g_{2}
. The bivariate quotient function is then defined as follows:

Definition 5.4
.

Given a function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, a set
B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, and a function
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, define the following function from
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}

{\sf Quo}_{2}(f,B,{\sf Fill})(x,y)=\begin{cases}\frac{f(x,y)}{V_{B}(y)}\quad\text{if }y\notin B\\
{\sf Fill}(x,y)\quad\text{if }y\in B.\end{cases}

(7)

In analogy to the univariate case, we have two lemmas regarding the proximities of the quotiented functions to respective Reed-Muller codes without side conditions. To state them, we introduce the notion of correlated agreement.

Definition 5.5
.

We say that two functions
g_{1},g_{2}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
have
\eta
-correlated agreement with degrees
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(d^{\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{2})
respectively if there exist functions
G_{1},G_{2}:\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
of degrees
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(d^{\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{2})
respectively and a set
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
of fractional size at least
\eta
such that

g_{1}|_{U}=G_{1}|_{U}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}|_{U}=G_{2}|_{U}.

Remark 5.6
.

The reason that
\eta
-correlated agreement with individual degrees is useful is that if
g_{1}
and
g_{2}

do not
have such correlated agreement, then we can combine them using
Lemma

4.11
and obtain, with high probability, a function that is far from having low individual degree.

With this in hand, the following lemma handles the completeness case and shows that the honest prover can provide functions
g_{1}
and
{\sf Fill}
which will result in a quotient with low individual degrees in the case that
f\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
:

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
f\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
, and recall that
\tilde{h}=\widehat{h}|_{\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}}
. Then there exist a
g_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
of degree
(d-|A|,d)
and
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
such that,

{\sf Quo}_{2}(f(x,y)-V_{A}(x)\cdot g_{1}(x,y)-\tilde{h}(x,y),B,{\sf Fill})\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d-|B|),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}].

Proof.

Let
\widehat{g}_{1},\widehat{g}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x,y]
be the functions from the decomposition in (
6
).
Take
g_{1}=\widehat{g}_{1}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}
, and note that (after division)

\frac{f(x,y)-V_{A}(x)\cdot g_{1}(x,y)-\tilde{h}(x,y)}{V_{B}(y)}

is a polynomial, so we may take
{\sf Fill}
agreeing with it over
\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B
. It is clear that
g_{1}
is of the desired degree. As for,
{\sf Quo}_{2}(f(x,y)-V_{A}(x)\cdot g_{1}(x,y)-\tilde{h}(x,y),B,{\sf Fill})
, note that it is the evaluation of
\widehat{g}_{2}
from (
6
), so it is also of the desired degree.
∎

The next lemma handles the soundness case and is a bit more technical. Morally speaking, it says that
f\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
, then there is no way for the prover to provide a
g_{1}
and
{\sf Fill}
such that
g_{1}
and the quotient calculated by the verifier are consistent.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
h:A\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be a side condition function, with
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}
and recall that
\tilde{h}=\widehat{h}|_{\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}}
. Suppose that
Q:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfies

{\sf agr}(Q,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h])\leq\varepsilon.

Then for any
g_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
,
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, and
g_{2}={\sf Quo}_{2}(Q-V_{A}\cdot g_{1}-\tilde{h},B,{\sf Fill})
, the functions
g_{1},g_{2}
cannot have
\varepsilon+\frac{|B|}{q}
-correlated agreement with individual degrees
(d-|A|,d)
and
(d,d-|B|)
respectively.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that the claim is false, and let
G_{1},G_{2}
be the functions with individual degrees
(d-|A|,d)
and
(d,d-|B|)
with which
g_{1},g_{2}
have correlated agreement, and denote by
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
the set of points they agree on. Let
\widehat{G}_{1},\widehat{G}_{2}
be their low degree extensions and consider the degree
(d,d)
polynomial

\widehat{G}=\widehat{V}_{A}\cdot\widehat{G}_{1}+\widehat{V}_{B}\cdot\widehat{G}_{2}+\widehat{h},

as well as the following function over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
:

g=V_{A}\cdot g_{1}+V_{B}\cdot g_{2}+\tilde{h}.

We have that
\widehat{G}
agrees with
g
on
U
and
g
agrees with
Q
on
U\setminus\left(\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\right)
.
Thus,
\widehat{G}
has individual degrees
(d,d)
, agrees with
h
on
A\times B
, and agrees with
Q
on
U\setminus\left(\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\right)
. Noting that

\mu(U)-\mu\left(\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\right)>\varepsilon+\frac{|B|}{q}-\frac{|B|}{q}\geq\varepsilon,

we get a contradiction to the assumption that
{\sf agr}(Q,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[Q,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h])\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

As previously mentioned, the purpose of
Lemma

5.8
is to show that if
Q
is far from an individual Reed-Muller code with side conditions, then for any
g_{1}
and
{\sf Fill}
provided, the functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
cannot have high correlated agreement with the some low individual degrees. In order for this to be useful however, we would like to combine
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
into a single function, and show that their lack of correlated agreement results in a combined function that is far from low individual degree. This is achieved via
Lemma

4.11
:

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose that
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h])\leq\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon\geq 21\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
, and let
|B|\leq q^{1/4}
. Fix any
g_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, and take

g_{2}={\sf Quo}_{2}(f-V_{A}\cdot g_{1}-\tilde{h},B,{\sf Fill})

where
\tilde{h}=\hat{h}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}
and
\widehat{h}
is the low-degree extension of
h
over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}
. Then we have

\Pr\left[{\sf agr}({\sf combine}_{(d,d)}(g_{1},g_{2}),\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\geq 1.04\varepsilon\right]\leq\frac{{\sf poly}(d)}{q^{\prime}},

where the randomness is over the random choice of proximity generator coefficients in
{\sf combine}
.

Proof.

Fix any
g_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. By
Lemma

5.8
,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
can have correlated agreement at most
\varepsilon+\frac{|B|}{q}
with degrees
(d-|A|,d)
and
(d,d-|B|)
respectively. The result then follows from
Lemma

4.11
.
∎

A similar result holds for the total degree Reed-Muller code.

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose that
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h])\leq\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon\geq 22\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
, and let
|B|\leq q^{1/4}
. Fix any
g_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, and take
g_{2}={\sf Quo}_{2}(f-V_{A}\cdot g_{1}-\tilde{h},B,{\sf Fill})
where
\tilde{h}=\hat{h}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}
and
\widehat{h}
is the low-degree extension of
h
over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}

Then we have that

\Pr\left[{\sf agr}({\sf combine}_{d}(g_{1},g_{2}),\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\geq 1.04\varepsilon\right]\leq\frac{{\sf poly}(d)}{q^{\prime}},

where the randomness is over the random choice of proximity generator coefficients in
{\sf combine}
.

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to the proof of
Lemma

5.9
, and is hence omitted.
∎

5.3
Trivariate Quotienting

Likewise, quotienting can be extended to three variables in the natural way.

Definition 5.11
.

Given an oracle function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, a set
C\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, and a function
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times\mathbb{F}_{q}\times C\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, define the following function from
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}

{\sf Quo}_{3}(f,B,{\sf Fill})(x,y,z)=\begin{cases}f(x,y,z)\quad\text{if }z\notin C\\
{\sf Fill}(x,y,z)\quad\text{if }z\in C.\end{cases}

(8)

If a function
\widehat{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x,y,z]
agrees with some side condition
h:A\times B\times C\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, then by
Lemma

3.16
there exist functions
\widehat{g}_{1},\widehat{g}_{2},\widehat{g}_{3}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x,y,z]
such that

\widehat{f}=\widehat{V}_{A,1}\cdot\widehat{g}_{1}+\widehat{V}_{B,2}\cdot\widehat{g}_{2}+V_{C,3}\cdot\widehat{g}_{3}+\widehat{h}.

Using this decomposition, one can similarly define a combine function as follows and obtain an analogue of
Lemma

5.10
for the total degree trivariate Reed-Muller code, which we state below (there are analogs for for the individual degree case, but we do not require them).

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose that
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\;|\;h])\leq\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon\geq 1.02\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
, and let
|C|\leq q^{1/4}
and set
\tilde{h}=\widehat{h}|_{\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}}
. Fix any
g_{1},g_{2}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times\mathbb{F}_{q}\times C\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, and take
g_{2}={\sf Quo}_{3}(f-V_{A,1}\cdot g_{1}-V_{B,2}\cdot g_{2}-\tilde{h},C,{\sf Fill})
. Then we have

\Pr\left[{\sf agr}({\sf combine}_{d}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}),\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\geq 1.04\varepsilon\right]\leq\frac{{\sf poly}(d)}{q^{\prime}},

where the randomness is over the random choice of proximity generator coefficients in
{\sf combine}
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the proof of
Lemma

5.9
, and we omit the details.
∎

6
Poly-IOPs

In this section we present the Poly-IOP model, which is a version of an IOP with a promise for each prover message.
Following ideas from
[
ACY23
]
we also give a transformation that turns a Poly-IOP into a legitimate IOP by using low degree IOPPs.

6.1
The Poly-IOP model

A Poly-IOP is an idealized model of IOPs wherein the prover’s messages each round are promised to be from some prespecified error correcting code.

Definition 6.1
.

Fix a field
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. A Poly-IOP is an IOP in which each round
the verifier and prover interact, and at the end the verifier decides to accept or reject.

For each round of interaction,
i
, there is a list of families
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{k_{i}}
, where for each
i
and
j
we have that
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m_{i,j}}]
. During round
i
, the prover sends polynomials,
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,k_{i}
such that
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j},\forall j\in[k_{i}]
. We consider the following complexity parameters of Poly-IOPs:

•

Round complexity:
the number of rounds of interaction during the interaction phase.

•

Input query complexity:
the number of queries to the input oracle.

•

Proof query complexity:
for each polynomial sent by the prover,
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
, we record the number of queries,
{\sf Q}^{(i)}_{j}
, made to
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
, as well as the queried points,
z^{(i)}_{j,1},\ldots,z^{(i)}_{j,{\sf Q}^{(i)}_{j}}
.

•

Completeness:
the maximum probability, over all prover strategies, that the verifier accepts in the case that the input is in the language.

•

Round-by-round soundness:
defined the same way as in an IOP.

For our purposes,
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
will always be either a degree
d
Reed-Solomon Code, a degree
d
Reed-Muller code, or an individual degree
(d,d)
Reed-Muller code, for some degree parameter
d
. Thanks to the good distance of these codes, the soundness of Poly-IOPs is often much easier to analyze. At a high level, this is because if the prover sends a low degree polynomial that is different than the one intended, then the verifier can easily detect this by making a few random queries to the polynomial’s evaluation.

Having said that, while Poly-IOPs themselves are not legitimate IOPs, they can be compiled into legitimate IOPs. To facilitate that we need
to take a few points into consideration:

•

Length: the Poly-IOP polynomials are typically over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
with
q^{\prime}\gg n
, so to construct linear size IOPs, we cannot afford to send the evaluation of even a single polynomial.

•

Removing the promise: In an IOP, there is no way to force the prover to send functions from some fixed family, and we must find another way to (effectively) enforce this. To resolve this, we use an IOPP testing proximity to the families
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{k_{i}}
appearing in the Poly-IOP.

In the next section we describe how to resolve these issues via a process called compilation.

6.2
Compiling Poly-IOPs to IOPs using a Batched Code Membership IOPP

In this section we describe our compilation result,
Theorem

6.2
. Stating it requires some set up, which we now give.

Poly-IOP for a language:
\texttt{GenericPolyIOP}(\mathcal{L})
.

Suppose that
\texttt{GenericPolyIOP}(\mathcal{L})
is Poly-IOP for some language
\mathcal{L}
over the field
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
with the following guarantees:

•

Input: an instance
f
, which one should think of as an oracle function.

•

Completeness: if
f\in\mathcal{L}
, then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1.

•

Round-by-round soundness:
2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Initial State: the initial state is doomed if and only if
f\notin\mathcal{L}
.

•

Round complexity:
{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}
.

•

Input query complexity:
{\sf inpQ}_{{\sf poly}}

•

Proof query complexity: for each
i\in[{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}]
suppose there are
k_{i}
polynomials sent in round
i
,
\{\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j\in[k_{i}]}
, which are supposedly from the codes
\{\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j\in[k_{i}]}
. Suppose the verifier
queries the polynomial
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
at the
{\sf Q}_{i,j}
points
z^{(i)}_{j,1},\ldots,z^{(i)}_{j,{\sf Q}_{i,j}}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m_{i}}
.

Batched IOPP:
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
.

Suppose that
BatchedIOPP
is a batched code-membership IOPP that is tailored for
GenericPolyIOP
. By that, we mean that the codes that
BatchedIOPP
accepts and rejects depend on the
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
’s appearing in
GenericPolyIOP
as elaborated below. The codes that we will be testing are evaluations of some family of polynomials,
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
, over a subset of the domain,
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m}
. To this end, we define the code

\texttt{Eval}\left[\mathcal{C},U\right]=\{\widehat{f}|_{U}\;|\;\widehat{f}\in\mathcal{C}\}.

For
A\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m}
and a side condition function
h:A\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, we also define a version with side conditions

\texttt{Eval}\left[\mathcal{C},U\;|\;h\right]=\{\widehat{f}|_{U}\;|\;\widehat{f}\in\mathcal{C},\widehat{f}|_{A}=h\}.

For our purposes,
\mathcal{C}
is always a Reed-Solomon, total degree Reed-Muller, or individual degree Reed-Muller code. The IOPP
BatchedIOPP
has the following guarantees:

•

Input: For each
i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]
:

–

a proximity parameter
\varepsilon_{i,j}\geq 2\sqrt{\delta_{i,j}}
, where

\delta_{i,j}:=\max_{\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}}\Pr_{x\in H^{(i)}_{j}}[\widehat{F}(x)=\widehat{G}(x)],

–

a function
F^{(i)}_{j}:H^{(i)}_{j}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, where
H^{(i)}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m_{i}},

–

a side condition function,
h_{i,j}:{\sf Side}_{i,j}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, which maps
{\sf Side}_{i,j}=\{z^{(i)}_{j,1},\ldots,z^{(i)}_{j,{\sf Q}_{i,j}}\}
to values of the queries to
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
in the
GenericPolyIOP
.

–

The family
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
, from
GenericPolyIOP
.

•

Completeness: if for all
i\in[{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}],j\in[k_{i}]
, we have
F^{(i)}_{j}\in\texttt{Eval}\left[\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j},H^{(i)}_{j}\;|\;h\right]
, then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-round soundness:
2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Initial State: the initial state is doomed if and only if for at least one
i\in[{\sf rd}]
we have

{\sf agr}\left(F^{(i)}_{j},\texttt{Eval}\left[\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j},H^{(i)}_{j}\;|\;h\right]\right)\leq\varepsilon_{i,j}.

•

Round complexity:
{\sf rd}_{{\sf batch}}

•

Input query complexity:
{\sf inpQ}_{{\sf batch}}
.

•

Proof query complexity:
{\sf pfQ}_{{\sf batch}}
.

•

Length:
{\sf Len}_{{\sf batch}}
.

With this set up in hand, the compilation theorem now reads as follows:

Theorem 6.2
.

Suppose we have
\texttt{GenericPolyIOP}(\mathcal{L})
and
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
as described above. Let

\hat{\delta}_{i,j}:=\max_{\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}}\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m_{i,j}}}[\widehat{F}(x)=\widehat{G}(x)]\quad\text{and}\quad\delta_{i,j}:=\max_{\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}}\Pr_{x\in H^{(i)}_{j}}[\widehat{F}(x)=\widehat{G}(x)],

and suppose for all
i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]
we have

\varepsilon_{i,j}\geq 2\sqrt{\delta_{i,j}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\widehat{\delta}_{i,j}}{\varepsilon_{i,j}}\leq 2^{-\lambda+5\log(k_{i})}.

(9)

Then there is an IOP for the language
\mathcal{L}
with the following parameters

•

Completeness: if
f\in\mathcal{L}
then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-round soundness:
2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Initial State: the initial state is doomed if and only if
f\notin\mathcal{L}
.

•

Round complexity:
{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}+{\sf rd}_{{\sf batch}}+O(1)
.

•

Input query complexity:
{\sf inpQ}_{{\sf poly}}
.

•

Proof query complexity:
{\sf inpQ}_{{\sf batch}}+{\sf pfQ}_{{\sf batch}}+\sum_{i=1}^{{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}}k_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}{\sf Q}_{i,j}
.

•

Length:
{\sf Len}_{{\sf batch}}+\sum_{i=1}^{{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}|H^{(i)}_{j}|
.

We refer to this IOP as
\texttt{Compile}(\texttt{GenericPolyIOP}(\mathcal{L}),\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]}))
.

Remark 6.3
.

For all of our purposes in this paper, one can replace the proof query complexity in
Theorem

6.2
with
O({\sf inpQ}_{\texttt{BatchedIOPP}}+{\sf pfQ}_{\texttt{BatchedIOPP}})
. The reason is that the queries corresponding to the
\sum_{i=1}^{{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}}k_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{{\sf rd}_{{\sf poly}}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}{\sf Q}_{i,j}
term above end up each leading to at least one input or proof query in the batched IOPP.

High level overview of the compiled IOP:

we now describe the IOP in
Theorem

6.2
, which we call
Compile
in short. At a high level
Compile
has two main components: a Poly-IOP simulation phase, and a proximity test phase.

During the Poly-IOP simulation phase the prover and the verifier simulate
GenericPolyIOP
, except that instead of sending the entire polynomial
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
(which would be too large), the prover only sends its evaluation over some smaller domain
H^{(i)}_{j}
. The prover still answers queries over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m_{i,j}}
, however. One key addition the so called “anchoring” trick: as soon as the prover sends such an evaluation, the verifier performs an out-of-domain sample, asking for the supposed value of
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}(z^{(i)})
, for a randomly chosen
z^{(i)}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m_{i}}
.
The reason for that is that, with high probability, this out-of-domain sample anchors the prover, in the sense there will only be one member of
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
which has agreement at least
\varepsilon_{i,j}
with
F^{(i)}_{j}
that additionally agrees with the prover’s response to the out-of-domain sample. We denote this function by
\widehat{G}^{(i)}_{j}
.

From this point on, the prover has two options: they can either answer all of the queries in
GenericPolyIOP
to
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
according to
\widehat{G}^{(i)}_{j}
, or they can deviate from
\widehat{G}^{(i)}_{j}
(i.e. provide at least one answer that is inconsistent with
\widehat{G}^{(i)}_{j}
). In the former case, note that the verifier will reject by the soundness of
GenericPolyIOP
and the round-by-round soundness of the compiled IOP follows from that of
GenericPolyIOP
.

Now suppose it is the latter case that occurs. This is where the second phase comes into play. In that case, note that the oracle
F^{(i)}_{j}
does not have
\varepsilon_{i,j}
agreement with
any
member of
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
which also agrees with the side condition
h_{i,j}
. This is because the side conditions include both the out-of-domain sample and the point where the prover deviated from
\widehat{G}^{(i)}_{j}
. In this case
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
will reject and the round-by-round soundness of the compiled IOP then follows from that of
BatchedIOPP
.

Formal description of the compiled IOP:

formally, the IOP
Compile
proceeds as follows:

Poly IOP Compilation:
\texttt{Compile}(\texttt{GenericPolyIOP},\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]}))

1.

Poly-IOP Simulation Phase:

(a)

For
i\in[{\sf rd}]
:

i.

P:
The prover sends the oracle functions
F^{(i)}_{j}:H^{(i)}_{j}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, for
j\in[k_{i}]
. In the honest case, each
F^{(i)}_{j}
is the evaluation of the polynomial
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
which the honest prover in
GenericPolyIOP
would have sent.

ii.

V:
The verifier chooses points
z^{(i)}_{j}\in\mathbb{F}^{m_{i,j}}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random for each
j\in[k_{i}]
and sends all of these points to the prover.

iii.

P:
The prover sends
\zeta^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,\zeta^{(i)}_{k_{i}}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. In the honest case, these values agree with the polynomials that honest prover in
GenericPolyIOP
would have sent:

\zeta^{(i)}_{j}=\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}\left(z^{(i)}_{j}\right),\;\forall j\in[k_{i}].

iv.

V:
The verifier generates the set of queries that the verifier in
GenericPolyIOP
would have made during round
i
of
GenericPolyIOP
. They do so using the same randomness as the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
. For each
i^{\prime}\leq i
and each
j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
, the verifier sends the prover each point that the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
would have queried
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}
during round
i
of
GenericPolyIOP
. The verifier also sends the message that the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
would have sent during round
i
of
GenericPolyIOP
.

v.

P:
The prover sends a field element of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
in response to each query.

vi.

Both parties proceed to the next round of the Poly-IOP.

2.

Decision and Test Phase:

(a)

V:
The verifier simulates the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
and rejects if the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
would have. If this is the case, then the protocol terminates. Otherwise, both parties proceed to the next step.

During the simulation, for each query to
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
made by the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
, the current verifier acts as if the answer is what the prover provided during step iii above. Using these answers, the current verifier can then simulate the decision of the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
.

(b)

For each
i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]
let
{\sf Side}_{i,j}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m}
consist of the points where the verifier queries the
\widehat{F}_{i}
during step iv above, as well as the point
z^{(i)}_{j}
. Let

h_{i,j}:{\sf Side}_{i,j}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}

be the map consisting of the prover’s responses given in step v of the Poly-IOP interaction phase.

(c)

P + V:
Both parties run
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
with the following inputs for each
i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]
,

•

Functions
F^{(i)}_{j}
,

•

Side condition functions
h_{i,j}
,

•

Function Family
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
.

6.3
Analysis of
Compile
: the proof of
Theorem

6.2

We analyze the parameters of
Compile
below. The analysis of the round-by-round soundness is the most involved part, and is deferred to
Section

6.4
. The round complexity, input query complexity, proof query complexity, and length are easy to verify, and we next argue about the completeness.

In the completeness case, there is a strategy for the honest prover in
GenericPolyIOP
which will make the verifier in
GenericPolyIOP
accept with probability
1
. Let
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
be the polynomials that the honest prover in
GenericPolyIOP
sends. The honest prover in
Compile

will first simulate the honest prover in
GenericPolyIOP
during the Poly-IOP Simulation Phase. Specifically, they send
F^{(i)}_{j}=\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}|_{H^{(i)}_{j}}
agreeing with
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
for each
i,j
and they answer all queries in steps ii and iv according to the polynomials
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
. By the completeness of
GenericPolyIOP
, the verifier in
Compile
will not reject. As for the “Test Phase”, we have
F^{(i)}_{j}\in\texttt{Eval}[\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j},H^{(i)}_{j}\;|\;h_{i,j}]
for all
i,j
, and by the completeness case of
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
the verifier always accepts.

6.4
Round-By-Round Soundness for
Theorem

6.2

To show the round-by-round soundness for
Compile
we will go over each round of interaction and define the doomed states.

6.4.1
The Poly-IOP Interaction Phase

We fix a round
i
. For each
i^{\prime}\leq i,j^{\prime}\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
, let
h^{(i)}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
be side condition function for the polynomial
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}}
after round
i
. That is, the domain of
h_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
consists of all of the points where the verifier queries
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}}
and
h^{(i)}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
maps these points to the answers provided by the prover (during step v of the Poly-IOP Simulation Phase). Let
\mathcal{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}}
consist of all
\widehat{F}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}}
that satisfy

•

{\sf agr}\left(F^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}},\widehat{F}|_{H^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}}}\right)\geq\varepsilon_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
.

•

\widehat{F}
agrees with
h^{(i)}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
.

During round
i
of the Poly-IOP interaction phase, there are three rounds of interaction. We will go through each of them, define the state function afterwards, and show soundness for that round.

Interaction 1:

•

P:
The prover send the function
F^{(i)}_{j}:H^{(i)}_{j}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
.

•

V:
The verifier sends
z^{(i)}_{j}\in\mathbb{F}^{m_{i,j}}_{q^{\prime}}
.

State Function 1
.

The state is doomed if and only if the previous state was doomed and the following holds for every
j\in[k_{i}]
. There do not exist distinct
\widehat{F},\widehat{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
satisfying:

•

\widehat{F}(z^{(i)}_{j})=\widehat{F}^{\prime}(z^{(i)}_{j})
,

•

the evaluations of
\widehat{F},\widehat{F}^{\prime}
over
H^{(i)}_{j}
both have agreement at least
\varepsilon_{i,j}
with the function
F^{(i)}_{j}
.

In other words, the anchoring step was successful and there is at most one member of
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
agreeing with the prover’s answer to
z^{(i)}_{j}
and agreeing non-trivially with the evaluation over
H^{(i)}_{j}
that the prover provided. Note that if the state is doomed then we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|\leq 1
for all
j\in[k_{i}]
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose the previous state is doomed. Then for any
F^{(i)}_{j}
sent by the prover,
State Function

1
will be doomed with probability at least
1-2^{-\lambda}
over the verifier’s random choice of
z^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,z^{(i)}_{k_{i}}
.

Proof.

Assume that the previous state was doomed. For each
F^{(i)}_{j}
sent by the prover, let

{\sf List}_{\varepsilon_{i,j}}(F^{(i)}_{j})=\left\{\widehat{F}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}\;|\;{\sf agr}(F^{(i)}_{j},\widehat{F}|_{H^{(i)}_{j}})\geq\varepsilon_{i,j}\right\}.

The state will be doomed after the verifier’s randomness if and only if for every
j
, no two members of
{\sf List}_{\varepsilon_{i,j}}(F^{(i)}_{j})
agree on
z^{(i)}_{j}
.
By
Theorem

3.8
, we have that
\left|{\sf List}_{\varepsilon_{i,j}}(F^{(i)}_{j})\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{i,j}}{\varepsilon_{i,j}^{2}-\delta_{i,j}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\varepsilon_{i,j}}
. The probability that any two polynomials in
{\sf List}_{\varepsilon_{i,j}}(F^{(i)}_{j})
agree on a randomly chosen
z^{(i)}_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{m_{i,j}}
is at most
\widehat{\delta}_{i,j}
, so by a union bound with probability at least

1-\widehat{\delta}_{i,j}\cdot\left|{\sf List}_{\varepsilon_{i,j}}(F^{(i)}_{j})\right|^{2}\geq 1-\widehat{\delta}_{i,j}\cdot\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}_{i,j}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{k_{i}^{5}\cdot 2^{-\lambda}}

we have that no two members of the list agree on
z^{(i)}_{j}
. In order for the state to be doomed, we need this to hold for all
j\in[k_{i}]
. Thus, performing another union bound over
j\in[k_{i}]
, we get that the state is doomed with probability at least
1-\frac{k_{i}}{k_{i}^{5}\cdot 2^{-\lambda}}\geq 1-2^{-\lambda}
as desired.
∎

The second interaction and state function are as follows.

Interaction 2

•

P:
For every
j\in[k_{i}]
, the prover sends a field element
\zeta^{(i)}_{j}
. In the honest case
\zeta^{(i)}_{j}=\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}(z^{(i)}_{j})
.

•

V:
The verifier sends the prover a list of all points they query
\widehat{F}_{j}
at for all
j\leq i
. The verifier also sends their Poly-IOP message for the next round, if there is such a round.

While defining the next state function, let us assume that
State Function

1
is doomed. Indeed, this is the only case that matters as far as round-by-round soundness is concerned. If
State Function

1
is doomed, then for each
j\in[k_{i}]
, after the prover sends
\zeta^{(i)}_{j}
in the above interaction, there is at most one member of
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{j}
which evaluates to
\zeta^{(i)}_{j}
on
z^{(i)}_{j}
and has at least
\varepsilon_{i,j}
agreement with
F^{(i)}_{j}
over
H^{(i)}_{j}
. In this case,
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|\leq 1
, and if
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=1
, let
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
be the unique member in
\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}
.

State Function 2
.

After the verifier’s message, we call the state doomed if at least one of the following holds:

•

For each
i^{\prime}\leq i
and each
j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
,
|\mathcal{F}_{j}^{(i^{\prime})}|=1
and the corresponding state in
GenericPolyIOP
is doomed. More specifically, in this case, the prover has consistently responded according to a single polynomial
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}
for every round
i^{\prime}\leq i
thus far and every polynomial
j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
of that round. We define the state as doomed if and only if
GenericPolyIOP
would be doomed assuming that the prover in
GenericPolyIOP
answered with the polynomials
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}
for each round
i^{\prime}\leq i
thus far and each
j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
of that round.

•

For some
i^{\prime}\leq i,j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
,
|\mathcal{F}_{j}^{(i^{\prime})}|=0
. In this case, there is at least one
i^{\prime},j
for which the prover has not answered consistently according to a single member of
\mathcal{C}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}
.

The soundness of this round is discussed in the next lemma. It essentially follows from the soundness of the corresponding round in
GenericPolyIOP
.

Lemma 6.5
.

If the previous state was doomed, then the next state is doomed with probability at least
1-2^{-\lambda}
.

Proof.

As we assume the previous state was doomed, we have that for all
i^{\prime}<i,j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
,
|\mathcal{F}_{j}^{(i^{\prime})}|\leq 1
. If for any of these
i^{\prime},j
we have
|\mathcal{F}_{j}^{(i^{\prime})}|=0
, then the next state is automatically doomed and we are done, so suppose that
|\mathcal{F}_{j}^{(i^{\prime})}|=1
for all of these
i^{\prime},j
.

Since
State Function

1
is doomed, it follows that no matter what
\zeta^{(i)}_{j}
’s the prover sends, we will have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|\leq 1
for all
j\in[k_{i}]
. Once again, if for any
j\in[k_{i}]
we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=0
, then the next state is automatically doomed and we are done. Thus, assume that
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=1
for all
j\in[k_{i}]
henceforth.

In this case we are in the setting of item 1 of
State Function

2
and
State Function

2
is doomed with probability at least
1-2^{-\lambda}
by the round-by-round soundness of
GenericPolyIOP
.
∎

The final interaction and state function of the Poly-IOP simulation phase are the following.

Interaction 3:

•

P:
The prover responds with a field element in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
for each such query.

•

V:
The verifier sends an empty message.

State Function 3
.

The state is doomed if and only if at least one of the following holds

•

For all
i^{\prime}\leq i,j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
, we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=1
, and the corresponding state in
GenericPolyIOP
is doomed. In this case there is exactly one
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}
consistent the the prover’s answers so far, and the prover has continued to answer according to this
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}
in the present interaction.

•

For some
i^{\prime}\leq i,j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=0
. In this case there is some
i^{\prime},j
where the prover has not consistently answered according to a single
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}
.

In fact, regardless of how the prover responds here, the next state will be automatically doomed if the previous state was doomed. Informally, this is because the round-by-round soundness was handled by the verifier’s random choice of queries in the previous round. The simulation phase is designed so that once the verifier decides on the queries to the polynomials
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
’s, we have soundness regardless of how the prover decides to respond to these queries.

Lemma 6.6
.

If the previous state was doomed, then the next state is doomed with probability
1
.

Proof.

We assume that the previous state is doomed. If for some
i^{\prime}\leq i,j\in[k_{i^{\prime}}]
, we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=0
, then we are done. Otherwise, the prover has responded with values that agree with the unique
\widehat{F}^{(i^{\prime})}_{j}\in\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}
for each
i^{\prime},j
. In this case the second item of
State Function

3
is satisfied and the state is doomed.
∎

6.4.2
The Decision and Test Phase

The remainder of the round-by-round soundness follows from that of
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
. The last round of interaction before the parties run
BatchedIOPP
is the following.

Interaction 1:

•

V:
The verifier simulates the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
. For each query to
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
made by the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
, the current verifier acts as if the answer is that provided by the current prover during step iii of the Poly-IOP Simulation phase.

After this round, the state function is as follows.

State Function 4
.

The state is doomed if and only if one of the following holds

•

The verifier rejects and terminates the protocol.

•

For some round
i
and
j\in[k_{i}]
, we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=0
.

Lemma 6.7
.

If the previous round is doomed, then
State Function

4
is doomed with probability at least
1-2^{-\lambda}
.

Proof.

If for some round
i
and
j\in[k_{i}]
, we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=0
, then the state is automatically doomed and we are done. Otherwise, we have
|\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{j}|=1
for all rounds
i
and
j\in[k_{i}]
. In this case, the verifier will simulate the decision of the verifier from
GenericPolyIOP
assuming that the prover in
GenericPolyIOP
sent
\widehat{F}^{(i)}_{j}
for all rounds
i
and
j\in[k_{i}]
. The soundness of this round then follows from the round-by-round soundness of
GenericPolyIOP
.
∎

In the remainder of
Compile
, both parties run the IOPP
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
, and the round-by-round soundness therein follows from the assumed round-by-round soundness of
BatchedIOPP
. Indeed, one can check that if the state is doomed going into step c of the “Decision and Test phase” of
\texttt{Compile}(\texttt{GenericPolyIOP},\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]}))
, then one of the following holds:

•

The first item of
State Function

4
holds and the protocol is terminated.

•

The second item of
State Function

4
holds and the input to
\texttt{BatchedIOPP}(\{\varepsilon_{i,j}\}_{i\in[{\sf rd}],j\in[k_{i}]})
is such that the initial state of
BatchedIOPP
doomed.

In the first case, the protocol has terminated and there are no more rounds to analyze. In the second case, the remaining rounds have soundness error at most
2^{-\lambda}
due to the round-by-round soundness of
BatchedIOPP
.

7
IOPPs in the Low Rate Regime

In this section, we construct IOPs of Proximity for the degree
d
Reed-Solomon and individual degree
(d,d)
Reed-Muller codes in the low rate regime. By low rate, we mean that the degree parameter
d
is polynomially smaller than the field size
q
.
These low rate IOPPs have size that is roughly quadratic in
d
, making them unsuitable for our final application. Instead, they are used after we have already reduced the problem size by a square-root factor.

7.1
Low Rate Reed-Solomon IOPP

Fix a security parameter
\lambda
and a sequence of degrees of the form
2^{2^{k}}
for
k=1,\ldots,M
. Fix two finite fields
\mathbb{F}_{q},\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and

q>2^{2^{M}+1},\quad\quad q^{\prime}=2^{\lambda}\cdot{\sf poly}(q).

Furthermore, for each
k\in[M]
, set

\varepsilon_{k}=\left(\frac{2^{2^{k}+1}}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}/2},\quad\quad T_{k}=\Theta\left(\frac{\lambda}{\log(1/\varepsilon_{k})}\right).

Notice that for every
k\in[M]
, we have
T_{k}=O(\lambda/\log(q))
. With these parameters in mind, our IOPP for the Reed-Solomon code in the low-rate regime reads as follows:

Theorem 7.1

(Low Rate Reed-Solomon)
.

Fix a degree parameter
d=2^{2^{k}}
and let
\lambda,q,q^{\prime}
be as above and set an agreement parameter
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{k}
be as above. Then there is an IOPP to the Reed-Solomon code, which we refer to as
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,d,q,\lambda,\varepsilon)
, that has the following guarantees:

•

Input:
a function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
.

•

Completeness:
if
f\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
, then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-round soundness:

2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Initial state:
the initial state is doomed if and only if
{\sf agr}(f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
.

•

Round complexity:

O(k)=O(\log\log d)
.

•

Input query complexity:

{\sf inpQ}_{\RS,k}=O\left(\frac{\lambda}{\log(1/\varepsilon_{k})}\right).

•

Proof query complexity:

{\sf pfQ}_{\RS,k}=O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon_{i})}\right).

•

Length:

O(q^{2}\cdot k)=O(q^{2}\log\log d)
.

Proof.

Deferred to
Section

8
, after we establish preliminary results in
Sections

7.2
and
7.3
.
∎

Our low-rate IOPP for individual degree Reed-Muller codes can be stated as follows:

Theorem 7.2
.

(Low Rate Individual Degree Reed-Muller)
Fix a degree parameter
d=2^{2^{k}}
and let
\lambda,q,q^{\prime}
be as above and set an agreement parameter
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{k}
be as above. Then there is an IOPP to the Reed-Muller code with individual degrees
d,d
, which we refer to as
\texttt{iRM-IOPP}(f,(d,d),q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k})
, that has the following guarantees:

•

Input:
a function
f:\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
.

•

Completeness:
if
f\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}]
, then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-round soundness:

2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Initial state:
the initial state is doomed if and only if
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}^{2}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon
.

•

Round complexity:

O(k)=O(\log\log d)

•

Input query complexity:

{\sf inpQ}_{\iRM,k}=O\left(\frac{\lambda}{\log(1/\varepsilon_{k})}\right).

•

Proof query complexity:

{\sf pfQ}_{\iRM,k}=O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon_{i})}\right).

•

Length:

O(q^{2}\cdot k)=O(q^{2}\log\log d)
.

Proof.

Deferred to
Section

8
, after we establish preliminary results in
Sections

7.2
and
7.3
.
∎

The proofs of
Theorem

7.1
and
Theorem

7.2
:

we prove both results together by induction on
k
. Namely, to construct the IOPP
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,d_{k},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k})
and
\texttt{iRM-IOPP}(f,d_{k},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k})
we will assume that we have access to the IOPPs
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,d_{k-1},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
and
\texttt{iRM-IOPP}(f,d_{k-1},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
as per the statements of
Theorem

7.1
,
Theorem

7.2
respectively. Towards this end, in
Sections

7.2
and
7.3

we first construct appropriate Poly-IOPs. Then, in
Section

8
we compile them into proper IOPPs using
Theorem

6.2
. The inductive hypothesis is used in the compilation step.

7.2
Low Rate Reed-Solomon Poly-IOPP

Towards
Theorem

7.1
, we start by constructing a Poly-IOPP for testing proximity to
\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
.

Lemma 7.3
.

With the notation of
Theorem

7.1
, there is a Poly-IOPP with the following guarantee:

•

Input:
a function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
.

•

Completeness:
if
f\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
, then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-round soundness:

2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Initial state:
the initial state is doomed if and only if
{\sf agr}(f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon_{k}
.

•

Number of polynomials:

1
.

•

Input query complexity:

T_{k}
.

•

Proof query complexity:

T_{k}
.

Proof.

We begin by describing the Poly-IOPP.

IOP 1

Reed-Solomon Poly IOP:
\texttt{RS-Poly}(f,d,q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k})

1:

P:
The prover sends a polynomial
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d})}[x,y]
. In the honest case,

\widehat{Q}(x^{\sqrt{d}},x)=\widehat{f}(x),

where
\widehat{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x]
is the low degree extension of
f
. Let
S=\{(\alpha^{k},\alpha)\;|\;\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}\}
.

2:

V:
The verifier chooses
T_{k}
-points,
(\alpha_{i}^{\sqrt{d}},\alpha_{i})\in S,\forall i\in[T_{k}]
, uniformly at random and checks for each
i\in[T_{k}]
if

\widehat{Q}(\alpha_{i}^{\sqrt{d}},\alpha_{i})=f(\alpha_{i})

If any check fails then the verifier rejects. Otherwise the verifier accepts.

We show that
\texttt{RS-Poly}(f,d,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k})
satisfies the guarantees of
Lemma

7.3
. The round complexity, input query complexity, and proof complexity are straightforward to check.

To see the completeness, let
\widehat{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x]
denote the low degree extension of
f
. If
f\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}]
, then
\deg(\widehat{f})\leq d
. The honest prover can send
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d})}[x,y]
according to
Lemma

3.12
which satisfies
\widehat{Q}(x^{\sqrt{d}},x)=\widehat{f}(x)
. In this case it is clear that the verifier will accept with probability
1
.

For the round-by-round soundness, there is only one round of interaction. We define the state as doomed after this round of interaction if and only if one of the verifier’s checks fails and the verifier rejects. If
{\sf agr}(f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon_{k}
, then for any
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d})}
that the prover sends, note that

\Pr_{(\alpha^{\sqrt{d}},\alpha)\in S}[f(\alpha)=\widehat{Q}(\alpha^{\sqrt{d}},\alpha)]\leq\varepsilon_{k}.

Indeed, otherwise
f
would have agreement greater than
\varepsilon_{k}
with the degree
d
function
\widehat{Q}(x^{\sqrt{d}},x)
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. It follows that the verifier rejects with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{k}^{T_{k}}\geq 1-2^{-\lambda}
. The round-by-round sondness foloows.
∎

7.3
Low Rate Individual Degree Reed-Muller IOPP

Towards
Theorem

7.2
, we construct a Poly-IOP for testing proximity to
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}]
. In the Poly-IOP the prover will send a two univariate polynomials with degree at most
d
and one bivariate polynomial with total degree
2d
. We will ultimately compile this Poly-IOP into a suitable batched code membership IOP which relies on the degree
d
-Reed Solomon IOPP.

Lemma 7.4
.

There is a Poly-IOPP for proximity to the
(d,d)
-degree Reed-Muller code with the following guarantees:

•

Input:
a function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
.

•

Completeness
: if
f\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}]
, then the honest prover makes the verifier

•

Round-by-round soundness
:
2^{-\lambda}

•

Initial state:
the initial state is doomed if and only if
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\leq\varepsilon_{k}
.

•

Round complexity:

5
.

•

Input query complexity:

T_{k}
.

•

Proof query complexity:

T_{k}+4
.

Proof.

We begin with a formal description of the Poly-IOPP:

IOP 2

Individual Degree Reed-Muller IOP:
\texttt{iRM-Poly}(f,(d,d),q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k})

1:

P:
The prover sends
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{F}^{\leq 2d}_{q^{\prime}}[x,y]
. In the honest case,
\widehat{Q}
is the low degree extension of
f
.

2:

V:
The verifier chooses
T_{k}
points
a_{1},\ldots,a_{T_{k}}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
uniformly at random and checks

\widehat{Q}(a_{i})=f(a_{i}),\quad\forall i\in[T_{k}].

If any check fails then the verifier rejects.

3:

V:
The verifier chooses
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random and sends these to the prover.

4:

P:
The prover sends
\widehat{F}_{1},\widehat{F}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d}[x]
. In the honest case,

\widehat{F}_{1}(x)=\widehat{Q}(x,\beta)\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{F}_{2}(y)=\widehat{Q}(\alpha,y).

5:

V:
The verifier chooses
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random and checks if

\widehat{F}_{1}(\zeta_{1})=\widehat{Q}(\zeta_{1},\beta)\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{F}_{2}(\zeta_{2})=\widehat{Q}(\alpha,\zeta_{2}).

If either check fails, then the verifier rejects. Otherwise, the verifier accepts.

At a high level
the Poly-IOP is doing two checks to handle two separate cases in the soundness case. First, the prover may send
\widehat{Q}
which as total degree
2d
and individual degrees at most
(d,d)
. In this case,
\widehat{Q}
should disagree with
f
on at most
\varepsilon_{k}
-fraction of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
, and the verifier will catch this with high probability in step 2. In the second case, the prover may send
\widehat{Q}
has one variable with degree between
d+1
and
2d
. This case is handled by steps 3 through 5. The verifier catches the prover here by randomly fixing each of the variables and comparing the resulting univariate polynomials (in
x
and
y
respectively)
\widehat{Q}(x,\beta)
and
\widehat{Q}(\alpha,y)
to degree
d
functions. Since one of these functions should have degree between
d+1
and
2d
, it will differ from every degree
d
function almost everywhere, so the verifier will reject with high probability in step 5.

We now give the formal analysis of the Poly-IOP.
In the completeness case,
f\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}]
. Let
\widehat{f}
be its low degree extension over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. The honest prover sends
\widehat{Q}=\widehat{f}
in the first step. In this case, it is clear that the verifier will not reject during the second step. During the fourth step, the honest prover sends
\widehat{F}_{1}(x)=\widehat{f}(x,\beta)
and
\widehat{F}_{2}(y)=\widehat{f}(\alpha,y)
. Since the honest prover also sent
\widehat{Q}=\widehat{f}
, it is clear that the checks in step 5 will pass with probability
1
. Overall, this establishes the completeness case. The round complexity and query complexities are easy to see, so we now argue the round-by-round soundness.

First Round of Interaction:

After the first round of interaction, i.e. after step 3, we define the state as doomed if and only if one of the following holds:

•

One of the checks in step 2 fails, in which case the protocol terminates and every state afterwards is doomed.

•

One of the univariate polynomials
\widehat{Q}(x,\beta)
,
\widehat{Q}(\alpha,y)
has degree greater than
d
.

If the initial state is doomed, then,
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(d,d),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\leq\varepsilon_{k}
. We consider two cases.

First suppose
\widehat{Q}
has individual degrees at most
d
. Then it follows that,

\Pr_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}[\widehat{Q}(a)=f(a)]\leq\varepsilon_{k}.

In this case, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{k}^{T_{k}}\geq 1-2^{-\lambda}
, one of the checks in step 2 will fail.

Now suppose
\widehat{Q}
does not have individual degrees at most
d
. Without loss of generality suppose it is the
x
-degree that is at least
d+1
. Then note that

\Pr_{\beta\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}\left[\deg(\widehat{Q}(x,\beta))\geq d+1\right]\geq 1-\frac{d-1}{q^{\prime}}\geq 1-2^{-\lambda}.

Indeed, consider the coefficient of the highest
x
monomial in
\widehat{Q}(x,y)
. This coefficient is a polynomial in
y
, and since the total degree of
\widehat{Q}(x,y)
is
2d
, it is a degree
d-1
polynomial in
y
, so the inequality above follows from the Schwartz-Zippel lemma.

This shows that the first round has soundness error at most
2^{-\lambda}
.

Second Round of Interaction:

After the second round of interaction, i.e. after step 5, the state is doomed if and only if one of the verifier’s checks in step 5 fails.

Suppose the previous state is doomed. We will show that for any message the prover sends in step 4, the next state will be doomed with probabilty at least
1-2^{-\lambda}
. If the verifier has already rejected, then the next state is automatically doomed and we are done. Thus, we suppose that one of
\widehat{Q}(x,\beta),\widehat{Q}(\alpha,y)
has degree at least
d+1
. Without loss of generality, suppose it is the former case. Then
\widehat{F}_{1}(x)\neq\widehat{Q}(x,\beta)
, and both have degree at most
2d
, so

\Pr_{\xi_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[\widehat{F}_{1}(\xi_{1})\neq\widehat{Q}(\xi_{1},\beta)]\geq 1-\frac{2d}{q^{\prime}}.

Overall, this shows that the state will be doomed with probability at least,
1-\frac{2d}{q^{\prime}}\geq 1-2^{-\lambda}
and completes our analysis of round-by-round soundness.
∎

8
Batched Code Membership IOPPs for Compiling to IOPs

In this section we give the inductive proofs of

Theorem

7.1
and
Theorem

7.2
. For the base cases, where
d=O(1)
, the IOPs are trivial, and we focus henceforth on the inductive step.

8.1
Set-up and an Auxiliary IOPP

For the inductive step let us suppose that we have the
k-1
cases of
Theorem

7.1
and
Theorem

7.2
. Namely, we assume that we have access to the IOPPs
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
and
\texttt{iRM-IOPP}(f,(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
that satisfy the guarantees in
Theorem

7.1
and
Theorem

7.2
respectively. In addition to these IOPPs, we will also assume access to an analogous IOPP for the degree
2\sqrt{d}
Reed-Solomon code that satisfies the following properties.

Lemma 8.1
.

Given
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
as in
Theorem

7.1
, one can construct an IOPP for degree
2\sqrt{d}
Reed-Solomon code, which we denote by
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,2\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
, that has the following properties:

•

Input:
a function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, an agreement parameter
\varepsilon_{k-1}>0
, and a degree parameter
2\sqrt{d}
which is a of the form
2^{2^{k-1}+1}
.

•

Completeness:
If
\deg(f)\leq 2\sqrt{d}
, then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-round soundness:

\lambda
.

•

Initial state:
the initial state is doomed if and only if

{\sf agr}(f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[2\sqrt{d},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon_{k-1}
.

•

Round complexity:

O(k-1)
.

•

Input query complexity:

{\sf inpQ}_{\RS,2\sqrt{d}}=O\left(\frac{\lambda}{\log(1/\varepsilon_{k-1})}\right).

•

Proof query complexity:

{\sf pfQ}_{\RS,2\sqrt{d}}=O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon_{i})}\right).

•

Length:

O\left((k-1)\cdot q^{2}\right)
.

Proof.

The idea is that the prover sends
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq\sqrt{d}}[x]
which supposedly satisfies
f_{1}+x^{\sqrt{d}}\cdot f_{2}=f
, and then both parties run
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f_{1}+\xi\cdot f_{2},\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
for a
\xi\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
that the verifier chooses randomly.
We defer the formal proof to
Appendix

D
.
∎

To complete the proofs of the inductive steps in
Theorem

7.1
and
Theorem

7.2
, we will apply
Theorem

6.2
and compile the Poly-IOPs from
Lemma

7.3
and
Lemma

7.4
into legitimate IOPPs. The only task left is to construct the batched code membership IOPPs required by
Theorem

6.2
. These IOPPs are nearly the ones assumed to exist by the inductive assumption. The only subtlety that arises is that we will need to test proximity to Reed-Solomon or Reed-Muller codes with side conditions. Hence the main work in this section is reducing from IOPPs for codes with side conditions to IOPPs for codes without side conditions,
and we use the tools from
Section

5
.

8.2
Compiling
RSPoly
for
Theorem

7.1

From
Lemma

7.3
, we already have a Poly-IOP that performs the task of
Theorem

7.1
. To complete the proof, we will construct a batched code membership IOPP,
RSBatch
, and use it to compile the Poly-IOP
RSPoly
into a legitimate IOP, satisfying
Theorem

7.1
. Our final IOPP will be
\texttt{Compile}(\texttt{RS-Poly},\texttt{RSBatch}(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k-1}),\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k-1})
, for

\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k-1}=0.9\varepsilon_{k-1}-\frac{T_{k}+1}{q}.

(10)

In the Poly-IOP,
RSPoly
, the prover sends one polynomial,
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d})}[x,y]
. When compiling
RSPoly
to an IOP, the IOP prover will instead send a function
Q:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, which is supposedly the evaluation of
\widehat{Q}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
. The verifier will query
\widehat{Q}
at
T_{k}+2
points in total: the out-of-domain sample
a_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}
, and the Poly-IOP queries
a_{1},\ldots,a_{T_{k}}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
. The prover will provide a value for
\widehat{Q}(a_{0})
, while the verifier can query the oracle
Q
for the values
\widehat{Q}(a_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{Q}(a_{T_{k}})
. Let
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be sets of size
T_{k}+1
such that
a_{0},\ldots,a_{T_{k}+1}\in A\times B
. In more detail,
A
consists of all of the
x
-coordinates of the
a_{i}
’s, while
B
consists of the
y
-coordinates. Let
h:\{a_{0},\ldots,a_{T_{k}+1}\}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
be the side condition function which agrees with the values
\widehat{Q}(a_{0}),\ldots,\widehat{Q}(a_{T_{k}})
provided by the prover. Let
\widehat{h}
be the low degree extension of
h
to
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}
that agrees with
\widehat{Q}
on
A\times B
. That is, we may first extend
h
to the entire subcube
A\times B
in a manner that agrees with
\widehat{Q}
, and then take the low degree extension to
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}
. Note that
\widehat{h}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{(T_{k},T_{k})}[x,y]
, so the prover can send
\widehat{h}
by giving its evaluation over
A\times B
and then having the verifier calculate the low degree extension.

To complete the proof the inductive step and conclude
Theorem

7.1
, it remains to show how to construct the code membership IOPP with side conditions for the oracle function
Q
with side conditions according to the queries above. Specifically, we must test proximity of
Q
to
\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
, and we will do so with the help of
Lemma

5.9
which allows us to reduce to the case without side conditions case:

IOP 3

Batched IOP for Reed-Solomon:
RSBatch

1:

P:
The prover sends
h:A\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, that agrees with the side conditions, where
A\times B
is the smallest subcube containing all of the side condition points. That is, they only provide the values of
h
at
A\times B\setminus\{a_{0},\ldots,a_{T_{k}+1}\}
, while the verifier assumes that the remaining values of
h
agree with the side conditions. The prover also sends a function
g_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and a function
{\sf Fill}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\times B\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
.

2:

Let

g_{2}={\sf Quo}(f-V_{A}\cdot g_{1}-\tilde{h},B,{\sf Fill}),

where
\tilde{h}
is the degree
(T_{k},T_{k})
-extension of
h
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
. In the honest case,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are as
Lemma

5.7
and
h=\widehat{Q}|_{A\times B}
.

3:

V:
The verifier chooses
F={\sf combine}_{(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d})}(g_{1},g_{2})
as in
Lemma

4.11
.

4:

P + V:
Both parties run
\texttt{iRM-IOPP}(F,(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
.

We will now show that
IOP

3
satisfies the requirements outlined in
Theorem

6.2
. This will show that
\texttt{Compile}(\texttt{RSPoly}(f,d,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k}),\texttt{RSBatch})
satisfies the guarantees of
Theorem

7.1
, thereby completing the proof of
Theorem

7.1
.

Lemma 8.2
.

The IOP
RSBatch
has the following guarantees.

•

Input:
a function
Q:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and a side condition function
h
as described above.

•

Completeness:
if
Q\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h]
, then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-round soundness:

2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Initial state:
the initial state is doomed if and only if
{\sf agr}(Q,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;h])\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k-1}
.

•

Round complexity:

O(k)
.

•

Input query complexity:

O(T_{k})
.

•

Proof query complexity:

{\sf pfQ}_{\iRM,k-1}+O({\sf inpQ}_{\iRM,k-1})+(T_{k}+2)^{2}
.

•

Length:

O\left(k\cdot q^{2}\right)

Proof.

We discuss all of the guarantees except for round-by-round soundness, which we save for the next section. The round complexity, input query complexity, proof query complexity, and length are clear. In particular, they from the inductive assumption that
iRM-IOPP
satisfies
Theorem

7.2
. For the proof query complexity, note that there are
{\sf pfQ}_{\iRM,k-1}
proof queries when running
\texttt{iRM-IOPP}(F,(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
. Furthermore, the
{\sf inpQ}_{\iRM,k-1}
input queries to
F
, become proof queries. Note that each query to
F
can be simulated by
O(1)
oracle queries. We now argue the completeness.

In step 1, the honest prover completes the side condition function according to
\widehat{Q}
by sending
h=\widehat{Q}|_{A\times B}
. They also send
g_{1}
and
{\sf Fill}
according to
Lemma

5.7
. By
Lemma

5.7
,
F={\sf combine}_{(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d})}(g_{1},g_{2})\in\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d})]
and the remainder of the completeness follows from that of
\texttt{iRM-IOPP}(F,(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
.
∎

8.2.1
Round-by-Round Soundness for
Lemma

8.2

There is only one round of interaction, after which we define the state as doomed if and only if

{\sf agr}(F,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}),\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\leq\varepsilon_{k-1}.

(11)
