|(\mathbb{F}_{q}\times S_{2})\cap H|\geq|H|/2=\gamma\cdot q^{2}
,

•

For every
b\in S_{2}
, we have
|\{a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;(a,b)\in H\}|\geq\gamma\cdot q
,

•

For every
b\in S_{2}
we have
|\{a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}\;|\;(a,b)\in S_{1}\}|\geq\gamma/2\cdot|S_{1}|
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
[
HKSS24
, Lemma A.2]
.
∎

For the next lemma, denote
N_{d,D}
to be the number of exponent vectors,
(i,j,k)
that have
(1,1,d)
-weighted degree at most,
D
, and have
k\neq 0\pmod{2}
.

Lemma A.13
.

Suppose
D
satisfies
N_{d,D}>r(D+1)
. Then there is a nonzero
A\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x,y,z]
with
(1,1,d)
-degree at most
D
satisfying:

•

A(\alpha,y,T[L^{(2)}_{a}](y))=0\quad\forall\alpha\in S_{1}
,
as a univariate polynomial in
y
.

•

A
is supported on monomials whose
z
-degree is either
0
or odd.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
[
HKSS24
, Lemma A.7]
.
∎

Now fix
A
to be the polynomial satisfying
Lemma

A.13
. The next
lemma is analogous to
[
HKSS24
, Lemma A.8]
.
The proof is the same, but as the statement therein is written for
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-valued functions, we repeat it here for
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
-valued functions. The only difference is that we apply the Schwartz-Zippel lemma over the sub-domain
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{2}
.

Lemma A.14
.

The polynomial
A
also satisfies
A(\alpha,\beta,f(\alpha,\beta))=0
for all
(\alpha,\beta)\in S:=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in H\;|\;\beta\in S_{2}\}
.

Proof.

Fix a
\beta\in S_{2}
, and consider the polynomial
P(x)=A(x,\beta,T[L_{\beta}^{(2)}](x))
. For all
\alpha\in S_{1}
such that
(\alpha,\beta)\in H
we

T[L_{\beta}^{(2)}](\alpha,\beta)=T[L_{\alpha}^{(1)}](\alpha,\beta)=f(\alpha,\beta),\qquad\qquad P(\alpha)=A(\alpha,\beta,T[L_{\beta}^{(2)}](x))=0.

Thus,
P
is equal to
0
on the entire set
\{\alpha\;|\;(\alpha,\beta)\in H,\alpha\in S_{1}\}
, which has size at least
\gamma|S_{1}|/2>D
. On the other hand
P
has degree at most
D
, so by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma it follows that
P
is identically
0
. Thus,

P(\alpha)=A(\alpha,\beta,T[L_{\beta}^{(2)}](x)(\alpha))=A(\alpha,\beta,f(\alpha,\beta))=0,

for all
\alpha
such that
(\alpha,\beta)\in H
. As this holds for all
\beta\in S_{2}
, the lemma is established.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

A.9
following the proof of
[
HKSS24
, Theorem 4.3]
.

Proof of
Theorem

A.9
.

Take
H\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
to be the set of points from
Claim

A.11
and let
\gamma=\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})
be such that
2\gamma\cdot q^{2}=|H|
. Applying an affine transformation if necessary, we assume that the directions from
Claim

A.11
are the standard axes. Following
[
HKSS24
]
, we set
r=900d/\gamma^{2}
and
D=\gamma r/3
so that the following hold:

•

D>20d
,

•

|N_{d,D}|\geq D^{3}/12d=\frac{(300^{3}d^{2})}{12\gamma^{3}}
,

•

r(D+1)\leq 2rD=\frac{6\cdot(300)^{2}d^{2}}{\gamma^{3}}
,

•

\gamma r/2=450d/\gamma

We can then apply
Lemma

A.12
. Let
S_{1},S_{2}
be the sets therein and let

S=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;b\in S_{2},(a,b)\in H\}.

Since
\frac{D^{3}}{12d}>r(D+1)
and
D<\gamma r/2
, we have that there is a non-zero polynomial
\tilde{A}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x,y,z]
such that
\deg_{1,1,d}(\tilde{A})\leq D
, all monomials appearing in
\tilde{A}
are from the set
N_{d,D}
, and
\tilde{A}(a,b,f(a,b))=0
for all
(a,b)\in S
.

To conclude, we apply
[
HKSS24
, Claim A.9]
, which shows that
\tilde{A}
must depend on the variable
z
and take
A\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}[x,y,z]
to be the polynomial with minimum
(1,1,d)
-degree which vanishes on the set
\{(a,b,f(a,b)\;|\;(a,b)\in S\}
and has
\partial_{z}(A)\neq 0
. Note that by
Lemma

A.5
, we get that
{\sf Disc}_{z}(A)
is nonzero, and
A
satisfies the desired properties.
∎

A.3
Getting Agreement with the Lines Table

In this section we show how to conclude a degree
d
function that agrees non-trivially with the lines table. The result is given in
Theorem

A.15
and is a key step in the proof of
Theorem

A.1
. One important feature is that the agreement with the lines table is boosted in
Theorem

A.15
. Indeed, one goes from
\Omega(\varepsilon^{6})
agreement with the points table in
Theorem

A.1
to
\Omega(\varepsilon)
agreement with the lines table in
Theorem

A.15
.

As it will be helpful for us later on, we also allow the lines table in
Theorem

A.15
to be randomized.

Theorem A.15
.

Let
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
an oracle function and let
T
be a randomized lines table of degree
d
such that

\Pr_{L,T[L],x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)]=\varepsilon\geq\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}.

Then there exists a degree
d
function
F:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfying,

\Pr_{L,T[L]}[T[L]=F|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{5}.

By a randomized lines table of degree
d
,
T
, we mean that
T
has entries indexed by affine lines
L\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
, and for each affine line
L
,
T[L]
is a randomly distributed degree
d
function from
L\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
.

Towards the proof of
Theorem

A.15
, we will first show a list decoding version of
Theorem

A.7
. The proof proceeds using standard techniques. Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\Omega(\varepsilon^{6})
. Recall that we assume
\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq 2\sqrt{d/q}
.

Theorem A.16
.

Suppose the setup of
Theorem

A.15
. Then there is a list of
M\leq\frac{5}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
degree
d
functions
\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{M}\}
from
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, such that

\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2},L\ni x}\left[T[L](x)=f(x)\land f(x)\notin\{F_{i}(x)\}_{i=1}^{M}\right]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

and for each
i\in[M]
,

\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}[f(x)=F_{i}(x)]\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{4}
.

Proof.

Let
\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{M}\}
be the list of degree
d
polynomials that agree with
f
on at least
\varepsilon^{\prime}/4
-fraction of the points in
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
. By
Theorem

3.8
,

M\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}/4}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}/16-d/q}\leq\frac{5}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}.

We will show that
\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{M}\}
is the desired list of functions. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this is not the case. That is, suppose

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2},L\ni x\\
T[L]\end{subarray}}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land f(x)\notin\{F_{i}(x)\}_{i=1}^{M}]>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

(23)

For each
i\in[M]
, let
W_{i}=\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;f(x)=F_{i}(x)\}
. Now consider the
function
f^{\prime}
obtained by setting
f^{\prime}(x)=x_{1}^{d+1}
for every
x\in\cup_{i=1}^{M}W_{i}
and keeping
f^{\prime}(x)=f(x)
for all other points
x
. Note that
f^{\prime}
has agreement at most
0.26\varepsilon^{\prime}
with every degree
d
function over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
. Indeed, fix an arbitrary
g:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
of degree
d
, then

\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}[g(x)=f^{\prime}(x)]\leq\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}[g(x)=x_{1}^{d+1}]+0.25\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq 0.25\varepsilon^{\prime}+\frac{d+1}{q}\leq 0.26\varepsilon^{\prime}.

where we use the Schwartz-Zippel lemma in the second inequality.

On the other hand, by (
23
),
T
and
f^{\prime}
will pass with probability at least
\varepsilon/2
due to the points
x\notin\cup_{i=1}^{M}W_{i}
alone, so

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2},L\ni x\\
T[L]\end{subarray}}[T[L](x)=f^{\prime}(x)]>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Thus, we can fix for each
L
a choice of
T[L]
so that the line table becomes deterministic and the above inequality still holds.
Theorem

A.7
then implies that there is a degree
d
polynomial with agreement at least
\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{6}\right)\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}

with
f^{\prime}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

A.15

Proof of
Theorem

A.15
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{M}
be the degree
d
functions given by
Theorem

A.16
. For each
i
, let
W_{i}=\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;f(x)=F_{i}(x)\}
and let
W=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}W_{i}
.

For an affine line
L
and randomized lines table entry
T[L]
, we let
E_{i}
denote the event that
T[L]=F_{i}|_{L}
, and let Let
E=E_{1}\lor\ldots\lor E_{M}
. Then we can rewrite the pass probability from
Theorem

A.15
as:

\displaystyle\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land E\land x\in W]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land\overline{E}\land x\in W]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land x\notin W]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon.

We will show that the first term must be non-trivial and then further dissect it. To this end, we upper bound the second term and third terms from the right hand side above. For the third term, we apply
Theorem

A.16
to obtain

\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land x\notin W]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

For the second term we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land\overline{E}\land x\in W]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)\in\{F_{1}(x),\ldots,F_{M}(x)\}\land\overline{E}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=F_{i}(x)\;|\;T[L]\neq F_{i}|_{L}]

\displaystyle\leq M\cdot\frac{d}{q}

In the second transition, we perform a union bound and in the third transition we use the Schwartz-Zippel lemma along with the fact that
T[L]
and
F_{i}
are distinct degree
d
functions on the affine line
L
.

It follows that

\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}\left[T[L](x)=f(x)\land E\land x\in W\right]\geq\varepsilon-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}-M\cdot\frac{d}{q}\geq 0.49\varepsilon.

Performing a union bound on the above probability, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}\left[T[L](x)=f(x)\land T[L]=F_{i}|_{L}\land f(x)=F_{j}(x)\right]\geq 0.49\varepsilon.

Fix
i\neq j
and consider a term in the summation above. We can bound the term by

\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}\left[T[L](x)=f(x)\land T[L]=F_{i}|_{L}\land f(x)=F_{j}(x)\right]\leq\Pr_{x}\left[F_{i}(x)=F_{j}(x)\right]\leq\frac{d}{q}.

It follows that

\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}\left[T[L](x)=f(x)\land T[L]=F_{i}|_{L}\land f(x)=F_{j}(x)\right]\geq 0.49\varepsilon-\frac{M^{2}d}{q}\geq 0.48\varepsilon

For each
i
, let
\mathcal{F}_{i}:G(m,1)\xrightarrow{}[0,1]
be the function given by
\mathcal{F}_{i}[L]=\Pr_{T[L]}[T[L]=F_{i}|_{L}]
. Suppose
x
is sampled first, and then
L\ni x
, and finally
T[L]
. We can upper bound the probabilities in the summation as

\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{x}[x\in W_{i}]\cdot\Pr_{x\in W_{i},L\ni x,T[L]}[T[L]=F_{i}|_{L}]=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{x}[x\in W_{i}]\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in W_{i},L\ni x}[\mathcal{F}_{i}(L)].

Finally, because each
W_{i}
consists of at least
\varepsilon/4
-fraction of points, applying
Lemma

2.4
on the line versus point inclusion graph (with the singular value estimate from
Lemma

2.5
), we have

\left|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L}[\mathcal{F}_{i}(L)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in W_{i},L\ni x}[\mathcal{F}_{i}(L)]\right|\leq\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon/4}}

Putting everything together, we get that

\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{x}\left[x\in W_{i}\right]\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L}[\mathcal{F}_{i}(L)]\geq 0.48\varepsilon-\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon/4}}\geq 0.47\varepsilon.

It follows that there exists some
i
such that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L,T[L]}[\mathcal{F}_{i}(L)]\geq 0.47\varepsilon\cdot\left(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{x}\left[x\in W_{i}\right]\right)^{-1},

and we fix this
i
henceforth.

To conclude we note that the extra factor on the right hand side cannot be too large because the
W_{i}
’s have pairwise small intersections. Indeed,

1\geq\Pr_{x}\left[x\in W\right]\geq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{x}\left[x\in W_{i}\right]-\sum_{i\neq j}\Pr_{x}\left[x\in W_{i}\cap W_{j}\right].

For fixed,
i\neq j
,
\Pr_{x}\left[x\in W_{i}\cap W_{j}\right]\leq\frac{d}{q}
. It follows that,

\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Pr_{x}\left[x\in W_{i}\right]\leq 1+\frac{M^{2}d}{q}\leq 2.

As a result, for the degree
d
function
F_{i}
, we have

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L,T[L]}[F_{i}(L)]=\Pr_{L,T[L]}[T[L]=F_{i}|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{5},

as desired.
∎

A.4
Boosting agreement to
\Omega(\varepsilon)
and the Proof of
Theorem

A.1

We are now ready to finish the proof of
Theorem

A.1
. At this point, most of the work has been done already and we conclude the proof with the following two steps:

•

apply
Theorem

A.15
to get agreement
\Omega(\varepsilon)
,

•

apply
Lemma

2.4
on the affine lines versus points graph to go from
\Omega(\varepsilon)
-agreement with the lines table to
\Omega(\varepsilon)
-agreement with the points table.

We now move on to the formal argument, and we begin with some set-up. Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

A.1
. For each line
L
, let

\varepsilon_{L}=\Pr_{x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)],\qquad\qquad\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\{L\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\;|\;\varepsilon_{L}\geq\varepsilon-(d/q)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}\}.

Then the relative size of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is at least
(d/q)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
, and

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land L\notin\mathcal{L}^{\prime}]\leq\varepsilon-\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}},

so

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land L\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}]\geq\varepsilon-\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)\land L\notin\mathcal{L}^{\prime}]\geq\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}.

(24)

Now consider the lines table
T^{\prime}
which is constructed as follows. For
L\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
, set
T^{\prime}[L]=T[L]
. For
L\notin\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
, set
T^{\prime}[L]
to be a uniformly random degree
d
function from
L\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. We claim that there is a setting of
T^{\prime}
such that for any degree
d
function,
g:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, we have

\Pr_{L\notin\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}[g|_{L}=T[L]]\leq\frac{1}{q}.

To this end note that, for any subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
of size
|S|=|\mathcal{L}|/q
, and degree
d
function
g:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, we have

\Pr_{T^{\prime}}[g|_{L}=T^{\prime}[L],\;\forall L\in S]=q^{\prime-(d+1)|S|}.

Now we union bound over
q^{\prime d^{2}}\leq q^{\prime q^{2}}
degree
d
functions
g:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and the number of possible sets
S\subseteq\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
of size
|\mathcal{L}|/q
. We use the following bound on the number of such
S
,

\binom{|\mathcal{L}|}{|S|}\leq q^{2|S|}.

Altogether, we get that the probability there exists a degree
d
function
g
agreeing with
T^{\prime}
on
|\mathcal{L}|/q
of the lines
L\in\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
is at most

q^{\prime-(d+1)|S|}\cdot q^{2|S|}\cdot q^{\prime q^{2}}<1.

Therefore, there exists a table
T^{\prime}
such that for any degree
d
function
g:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, we have

\Pr_{L\notin\mathcal{L^{\prime}}}[g|_{L}=T^{\prime}[L]]\leq\frac{1}{q}.

(25)

We fix this
T^{\prime}
and show how to conclude
Theorem

A.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

Fix
T^{\prime}
as described above. Then,

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T^{\prime}[L](x)=f(x)]\geq\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T^{\prime}[L](x)=f(x)\land L\in\mathcal{L}]\geq\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}},

where we are using (
24
) for the second inequality. Applying
Theorem

A.15
, we have that there exists a degree
d
function
F:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
such that

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L}}[F|_{L}=T^{\prime}[L]]\geq 0.2\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}.

By (
25
), all but
1/q
-fraction of the lines on which
F
and
T^{\prime}
agree must actually be in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
. Thus,

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L^{\prime}}}[F|_{L}=T^{\prime}[L]]\geq 0.2\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}-\frac{1}{q}:=\eta.

(26)

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
be the set of
L
’s such that
F|_{L}=T^{\prime}[L]
. Since
\varepsilon_{L}\geq\varepsilon-(d/q)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
for all
L\in\mathcal{L^{\prime}}
we
get that
\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime},x\in L}[F(x)=f(x)]\geq\varepsilon-(d/q)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
.
Also, note that the relative size of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}
is at least
\eta(d/q)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
.
It follows from
Lemma

2.4
(with the singular value estimate from
Lemma

2.5
) that

\displaystyle\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}}[f(x)=F(x)]\geq\varepsilon-\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}-\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\eta(d/q)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}}}\geq\varepsilon-1.01\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}.

For the last inequality, we are using (
26
) and the fact that
\tau_{{\sf line}}\leq 1/4
.
∎

A.5
The
m=3
Case: Proof of
Theorem

A.2

Here we show how to derive
Theorem

A.2
from
Theorem

A.1
via a boosting argument, using an argument from
[
RS97
]
. Suppose we are in the setting of
Theorem

A.2
with function
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and lines table
T
which satisfy

\Pr_{L\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3},x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)]\geq\varepsilon,

for
\varepsilon
satisfying
\varepsilon^{6}\geq\sqrt{d/q}
. Let
\mathcal{P}
denote the set of affine planes
P\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}
. For each plane
P\in\mathcal{P}
, let
\varepsilon_{P}=\Pr_{L\subseteq P,x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)]
. Then
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{P\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}}[\varepsilon_{P}]=\varepsilon
,
and by an averaging argument, for at least
\varepsilon/2
fraction of planes
P\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}
, we have
\varepsilon_{P}\geq\varepsilon/2
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
denote the set of such planes, and let us now construct a planes table
T^{\prime}
as follows.
For each plane
P
, let
T^{\prime}[P]\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d}[x,y]
be the degree
d
polynomial on
P
that has the highest agreement with
f|_{P}
.

Lemma A.17
.

For
P\in\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
, we have

\Pr_{x\in P}[T^{\prime}[P](x)=f(x)]\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}-1.01\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}.

Proof.

Since
T^{\prime}[P]
is the degree
d
polynomial over
P
which has the highest agreement with
f|_{P}
, it suffices to show that there is such a polynomial with agreement at least
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}-1.01\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
with
f|_{P}
. This follows immediately from
Theorem

A.1
. Indeed, for
P\in\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
, we have

\Pr_{L\subseteq P,x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Therefore
f|_{P}
and the lines table over
P
obtained by taking
T[L]
for
L\subseteq P
pass the line versus point test with probability
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Applying
Theorem

A.1
, there is a degree
d
polynomial with agreement at least
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}-1.01\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}
with
f|_{P}
.
∎

Now consider the graph
G(\mathcal{P},E)
, whose vertices are
\mathcal{P}
and whose edges are
(P,P^{\prime})
such that

•

T[P]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}}=T[P^{\prime}]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}}
,

•

P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
.

We first show that
G
has many edges.

Lemma A.18
.

The graph
G
has
\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})\cdot|\mathcal{P}|^{2}
edges.

Proof.

First one can calculate that

\Pr_{P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}[P\cap P^{\prime}=\emptyset]\leq\frac{1}{q^{2}}.

Indeed, we have
|\mathcal{P}|=\frac{q^{3}(q^{3}-1)(q^{3}-q)}{q^{2}(q^{2}-1)(q^{2}-q)}=q(q^{2}+q+1)
. On the other hand,
P
and
P^{\prime}
do not intersect if and only if they are identical or parallel, so for each
P
, there are
q
possible choices
P^{\prime}
such that
P\cap P^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Therefore,

\Pr_{P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}[P\cap P^{\prime}=\emptyset]=\frac{q}{|\mathcal{P}|}\leq\frac{1}{q^{2}}.

Now suppose we choose
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
with non-empty intersection and
x\in P\cap P^{\prime}
. Let
I(P,x)
be the event that
P\in\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
and
T[P](x)=f(x)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{P,P^{\prime},x\in P\cap P^{\prime}}[I(P,x)\land I(P^{\prime},x)]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x}[\Pr_{P\ni x}[I(P,x)]^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr_{x,P\ni x}[I(P,x)]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}).

In the last transition we use the fact that
P\in\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
with probability
\Omega(\varepsilon)
, and conditioned on this,
T[P](x)=f(x)
with probability
\Omega(\varepsilon)
. It follows that for
\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})
pairs, of
P,P^{\prime}
, we have,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in P\cap P^{\prime}}[I(P,x)\land I(P^{\prime},x)]\geq\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}).

For such
P,P^{\prime}
, it must be that
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
, and

\Pr_{x\in P\cap P^{\prime}}[T[P](x)=T[P^{\prime}](x)]\geq\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})>\frac{d}{q}.

By the Schwartz-Zippel lemma,
T[P]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}}=T[P^{\prime}]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}}
and
(P,P^{\prime})
is an edge in
G
.
∎

Lemma A.19
.

The graph
G
is
\frac{d+1}{q}
-transitive, in the sense that for every non-edge
(P,P^{\prime})
we have that

\Pr_{P^{\prime\prime}}[(P,P^{\prime\prime}),(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in E]\leq\frac{d+1}{q}
.

Proof.

Fix
P,P^{\prime}
which are non-adjacent. If either
P\notin\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
or
P^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
, then

\Pr_{P^{\prime\prime}}[(P,P^{\prime\prime}),(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in E]=0.

Otherwise, it must be the case that
T[P]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}}\neq T[P]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}}
. In this case, suppose we choose
P^{\prime\prime}
randomly. Then with probability at most
1/q
we have that
P^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from the line
P\cap P^{\prime}
. If
P^{\prime\prime}
is not adjacent from this line, then the only way for
P^{\prime\prime}
to be adjacent to both
P
and
P^{\prime}
is to have

T[P]|_{P\cap P^{\prime\prime}}=T[P^{\prime\prime}]|_{P\cap P^{\prime\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad T[P^{\prime}]|_{P\cap P^{\prime\prime}}=T[P^{\prime\prime}]|_{P\cap P^{\prime\prime}}.

(27)

This implies that,

T[P]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}\cap P^{\prime\prime}}=T[P^{\prime}]|_{P\cap P^{\prime}\cap P^{\prime\prime}}.

(28)

If
P^{\prime\prime}\supseteq P\cap P^{\prime}
, then we cannot have
P^{\prime\prime}
adjacent to both
P
and
P^{\prime}
as we cannot have (
28
) hold. If
P\cap P^{\prime}\cap P^{\prime\prime}
is a point, then this point is uniformly random point in
P\cap P^{\prime}
, and by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma (
28
) holds with probability at most
d/q
. Overall this shows that if
P,P^{\prime}
are not adjacent then

\Pr_{P^{\prime\prime}}[(P,P^{\prime\prime})\in E\land(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in E]\leq\frac{d+1}{q}.\qed

Now we can use the following fact, asserting that almost transitive graphs can be made transitive by deleting a few edges.

Lemma A.20
.

Let
H=(V,E)
be a graph which is
\beta
-transitive. Then, one can delete delete at most
2\sqrt{\beta}|V|^{2}
edges from
H
to obtain a graph
H^{\prime}
which is transitive.

Proof.

This is
[
RS97
, Lemma 2]
.
∎

Combining
Lemma

A.18
,
Lemma

A.19
, and
Lemma

A.20
, we get that one can delete at most
2\sqrt{\frac{d+1}{q}}|\mathcal{P}|^{2}
edges from
G
to obtain a graph
G^{\prime}
which is transitive. Thus
G^{\prime}
is transitive and contains at least
\left(\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})-\sqrt{\frac{d+1}{q}}\right)|\mathcal{P}|^{2}
edges. Let
\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{k}
be the cliques whose union is
G^{\prime}
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{1}
be the largest clique. It follows that

\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})|\mathcal{P}|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{C}_{i}|^{2}\leq|\mathcal{C}_{1}|\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{C}_{i}|\leq|\mathcal{C}_{1}|\cdot|\mathcal{P}|.

Therefore,
|\mathcal{C}_{1}|\geq\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})|\mathcal{P}|
. The clique
\mathcal{C}_{1}
appears in
G
too, so
\mathcal{G}
has a clique of fractional size
\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})
. Going back to the table
T^{\prime}
, the clique
\mathcal{C}_{1}
corresponds to a large number of planes
P
, such that the functions
T[P]
for
P\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
all mutually agree, and each
P\in\mathcal{P}_{{\sf good}}
. We now show that one can interpolate a degree
d
function
Q
such that
Q|_{P}=T[P]
for all
P\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
. To do so, we first rely on the same result in the
q=q^{\prime}
case, which appears in
[
RS97
]
.

Lemma A.21

(Lemma 4
[
RS97
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a set of affine planes
P\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}
of fractional size
\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})
in
\mathcal{P}
. Suppose for each plane
P
, there is a degree
d
function
T[P]:P\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that for all
P_{1},P_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
T[P_{1}]|_{P_{1}\cap P_{2}}=T[P_{2}]|_{P_{1}\cap P_{2}}
. Then there is a degree
d
function
Q:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that
Q|_{P}=T[P]
for all
P\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

This is
[
RS97
, Lemma 4]
.
∎

We now handle the general
q^{\prime}
case:

Lemma A.22
.

There is a degree
d
function
Q:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
such that
Q|_{P}=T[P]
for all
P\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
.

Proof.

Note that one can view
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
as a vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. As
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, there is some
k
such that
q^{\prime}=q^{k}
, and there exist
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
such that each
\beta\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
has a unique decomposition as an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear combination of
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{k}
:

\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i}\cdot\Lambda_{i}.

For every
\beta\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, let
{\sf proj}_{i}(\beta)=\gamma_{i}
from the expansion above.

It follows that we can also view each function
T[P]:P\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, can also be viewed as a vector of
k
-functions from
P\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Specifically, we can write the polynomial expansion of
T[P]
as

T[P](x_{1},x_{2})=\sum_{i+j\leq d}\beta_{i,j}\cdot x_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{j},

where the
\beta_{i,j}
’s are coefficients from
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. Then writing each
\beta_{i,j}
as an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear combination of
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{k}
, we get

T[P](x_{1},x_{2})=\sum_{i+j\leq d}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\gamma_{\ell,i,j}\cdot\Lambda_{\ell}\cdot x_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{j}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\Lambda_{\ell}\sum_{i+j\leq d}\gamma_{\ell,i,j}\cdot x_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{j},

where the
\gamma_{\ell,i,j}
’s are all in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Thus, for each
P\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
and each
\ell\in[k]
, we write

T_{\ell}[P]=\sum_{i+j\leq d}\gamma_{\ell,i,j}\cdot x_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{j},\qquad\qquad T[P]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\Lambda_{\ell}\cdot T_{\ell}[P],

where each
T_{\ell}[P]:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
. One can check that
T_{\ell}[P](x)={\sf proj}_{\ell}(T[P](x))
, and as a result, the functions
T_{\ell}[P]
all mutually agree for
P\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Applying
Lemma

A.21
, for each
\ell\in[k]
, we get that there are degree
d
functions
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
, such that
Q_{\ell}|_{P}=T_{\ell}[P]

for all
P\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Taking
Q=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\Lambda_{\ell}\cdot Q_{\ell}
, we get that
Q:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
is a degree
d
function satisfying
Q|_{P}=T[P]

for all
P\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
.
∎

From here we are ready to conclude the proof of
Theorem

A.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

A.2
.

Take
Q
to be the degree
d
polynomial from
Lemma

A.22
. Then,

\Pr_{P\in\mathcal{C}_{1},x\in P}[Q(x)=f(x)]=\Pr_{P\in\mathcal{C}_{1},x\in P}[T[P](x)=f(x)]\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}.

By
Lemma

2.4
with the planes versus points graph (with the singular value estimate from
Lemma

2.5
), we have

\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}}[Q(x)=f(x)]\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}-\frac{q^{-1}}{\sqrt{\Omega(\varepsilon^{2})}}\geq 0.49\varepsilon-1.01\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}},

and we are done. In the last inequality we are using the fact that
\Omega(\varepsilon)\leq q^{-1/6}
.
∎

A.6
Agreement with the Lines Table when
m=3

In
Theorem

A.15
we showed how to go from
\Omega(\varepsilon^{3})
agreement with the points table to
\Omega(\varepsilon)
agreement with the lines table and obtain
Theorem

A.15
. For future arguments, we will require a similar result which gives agreement with the lines table (instead of the points table) in the
m=3
case.

Theorem A.23
.

Let
f:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
an oracle function and let
T
be a randomized lines table of degree
d
such that

\Pr_{L,T[L],x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)]=\varepsilon\geq\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}.

Then there exists a degree
d
function
F:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfying

\Pr_{L,T[L]}[T[L]=F|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{5}.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as that of
Theorem

A.15
except one replaces
Theorem

A.7
(which only works when
m=2
) with
Theorem

A.2
(which does works when
m=3
).
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for
Section

3.3.4

In this section, we will prove the theorems from
Section

3.3.4

via standard reductions from the soundness of the line versus point test in
Theorem

A.1
.

B.1
Proof of
Theorem

3.10

Fix the set up as in
Theorem

3.10
and let
\mathcal{L}
be the set of lines
L
such that

{\sf agr}(f|_{L},\RS_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq 1.01\varepsilon.

Construct the randomized lines table
T
as follows. If
L\in\mathcal{L}
, then set
T[L]
to be the degree
d
function that has the highest agreement with
f|_{L}
. Otherwise, set
T[L]
to be a uniformly chosen univariate random degree
d
function. It follows that

\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)]\geq\Pr_{L}[L\in\mathcal{L}]\cdot\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon^{2}\geq\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}.

Applying
Theorem

A.15
we get that there is a degree
d
function
H:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfying

\Pr_{L,T[L]}[T[L]=H|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{5}.

However, almost all of the contribution to this probability is from
L\in\mathcal{L}
. Indeed, if
L\notin\mathcal{L}
, then
\Pr_{T[L]}[T[L]=H|_{L}]\leq q^{-(d+1)}
, so

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},T[L]}[T[L]=H|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{5}-\Pr_{T[L]}[T[L]=H|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{5}-q^{-(d+1)}:=\varepsilon^{\prime}.

To conclude, we apply
Lemma

2.4
(with spectral value estimates from
Lemma

2.5
) to get that

\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}}[f(x)=H(x)]\geq\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[f(x)=H(x)]-\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\mu(\mathcal{L})}}\geq 1.01\varepsilon-\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\geq\varepsilon.

\Box

B.2
Proof of
Theorem

3.11

The proof is by contradiction. Suppose that

\Pr_{P\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}}[{\sf agr}(f|_{P},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\geq 1.2\varepsilon]>\frac{100}{\varepsilon^{2}q}.

We will show that
{\sf agr}(f,\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}])>\varepsilon
, contradicting the assumption of
Theorem

3.11
.

For each
P\in\mathcal{S}
, let
T^{\prime}[P]
denote the degree
d
function closest to
f|_{P}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of planes
P
satisfying
{\sf agr}(f|_{P},\RM_{q^{\prime}}[d,\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}])\geq 1.2\varepsilon
, so that
{\sf agr}(T^{\prime}[P],f|_{P})\geq 1.2\varepsilon
for all
P\in\mathcal{S}
. By assumption, we have
\mu(\mathcal{S})>\frac{100}{\varepsilon^{2}q}
. Consider the following distribution,
\mathcal{D}
, which outputs an affine line
L
and a degree
d
function from
L\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
:

•

Choose
P\in\mathcal{S}
uniformly at random.

•

Choose
L\subseteq P
uniformly at random.

•

Output the pair
(L,T^{\prime}[P]|_{L})
.

Using the degree
d
functions
T^{\prime}[P]
, for
P\in\mathcal{S}
, we can now generate a randomized lines table
T
as follows. For each
L
, the degree
d
function
T[L]
is chosen according to the marginal distribution of
\mathcal{D}
conditioned on the affine line being
L
. That is, for each line
L
and any degree
d
function
h:L\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
,

\Pr[T[L]=h]=\frac{\mathcal{D}(L,h)}{\mathcal{D}(L,\cdot)}.

If there is no plane in
\mathcal{S}
containing
L
, then
T[L]
is uniformly random over all degree
d
functions on
L
.

We claim that the randomized lines table
T
passes the line versus point test with
f
with probability close to
1.1\varepsilon
. Note that in this test, one chooses a line
L
uniformly at random, then
T[L]
according to the joint distribution of
\mathcal{D}
, and finally a uniformly random
x\in L
. Thus,
(L,T[L])
is almost distributed according to
\mathcal{D}
, save for the fact that
L
is not quite uniformly random in
\mathcal{D}
.

Claim B.1
.

\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)]\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}:=1.1\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
F\in L_{2}(G(\mathcal{L}))
be the function given by,

F(L)=\Pr_{x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)].

It is clear that all values of
F
are in the interval
[0,1]
. The probability we are interested in is then,

\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L}[F(L)].

Then,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{P\in\mathcal{S},L\subseteq P}[F(L)]=\Pr_{(L,T[L])\sim\mathcal{D},x\in L}[T[L](x)=f(x)]=\Pr_{P\in\mathcal{S},x\in P}[T^{\prime}[P](x)=f(x)]\geq 1.2\varepsilon.

where in the expectation
L
is chosen according to the marginal distribution of
\mathcal{D}
. Applying
Lemma

2.4
, we have

\left|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L}[F(L)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L\sim\mathcal{D}}[F(L)]\right|\leq\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\mu(\mathcal{S})}}.

It follows that

\Pr_{L,x\in L,T[L]}[T[L](x)=f(x)]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L}[F(L)]\geq 1.2\varepsilon-\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{100\varepsilon^{-2}q^{-1}}}=1.1\varepsilon.\qed

Applying
Theorem

A.23
, it follows that there exists a degree
d
function
H:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfying:

\Pr_{L,T[L]}[H|_{L}=T[L]]\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{5},

(29)

where
L
is uniform. We would like to conclude that,

\Pr_{(L,T[L])\sim\mathcal{D}}[H|_{L}=T[L]]=\Pr_{P\in S,L\subseteq P}[H|_{L}=T^{\prime}[P]|_{L}]

is large. To accomplish this we will apply the spectral sampling lemma again.

Claim B.2
.

We have,

\Pr_{P\in S,L\subseteq P}[H|_{L}=T^{\prime}[P]|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{10},

and as a consequence,

\Pr_{P\in S}[T^{\prime}[P]=H|_{P}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{20}.

Proof.

Let
F^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}\to[0,1]
be the function over affine lines of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}
given by

F^{\prime}(L)=\Pr_{T[L]}[H|_{L}=T[L]].

From (
29
), we have
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L}[F^{\prime}(L)]=\mu(F^{\prime})\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{5}
.
The probability of interest can be expressed as:

\Pr_{P\in S,L\subseteq P}[H|_{L}=T^{\prime}[P]|_{L}]=\Pr_{(L,T[L])\sim\mathcal{D}}[H|_{L}=T[L]]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L\sim\mathcal{D}}[F^{\prime}(L)]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{P\in\mathcal{S}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L\subseteq P}[F^{\prime}(L)].

so applying
Lemma

2.4
we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{P\in S,L\subseteq P}[H|_{L}=T^{\prime}[P]|_{L}]\geq\mu(F^{\prime})-\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\mu(S)}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{5}-\frac{\varepsilon}{10}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{10},

completing the first part of the claim. For the second part of the claim, it follows from an averaging argument that for
\frac{\varepsilon}{20}
-fraction of the planes
P\in S
, we have,

\Pr_{L\subseteq P}[H|_{L}=T^{\prime}[P]|_{L}]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{20}.

For such planes
P
, if
H|_{P}\neq T^{\prime}[P]
, then by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma, we would have

\Pr_{L\subseteq P}[H|_{L}=T^{\prime}[P]|_{L}]\leq\Pr_{a,b\in P}[H|_{P}(a)=T^{\prime}[P](a)]\leq\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{2}<\frac{\varepsilon}{20},

so it follows that
H|_{P}=T^{\prime}[P]
for
\frac{\varepsilon}{20}
-fraction of the planes
P\in S
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.11
.

Let
S^{\prime}
be the set of planes in
S
on which
H|_{P}=T^{\prime}[P]
. By
Claim

B.2
, we have that
\mu(S^{\prime})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{20}\cdot\mu(S)\geq\frac{5}{\varepsilon q}
. Meanwhile, by definition of
S
, for each
P\in S^{\prime}\subseteq S
, we have,

\Pr_{x\in P}[H(x)=T^{\prime}[P](x)=f(x)]\geq 1.2\varepsilon,

so evidently

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{P\in S^{\prime}}\Pr_{x\in P}[H(x)=f(x)]\geq 1.2\varepsilon.

Applying
Lemma

2.4
it follows that,

\displaystyle\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{3}}[H(x)=f(x)]\geq 1.2\varepsilon-\frac{q^{-1}}{\sqrt{\mu(S^{\prime})}}\geq 1.2\varepsilon-\frac{q^{-1}}{\sqrt{5\varepsilon^{-1}q^{-1}}}>\varepsilon.

This contradicts the assumption of
Theorem

3.11
and completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for
Section

4.2

In this section we prove the theorems in
Section

4.2
and
Section

4.3
, and to do so we first show an auxiliary result for the total degree Reed-Muller code.

Claim C.1
.

Suppose the setting of
Theorem

4.4
and set
\delta\geq 1/\sqrt{q}
. Then for at least
1-\frac{2}{\delta^{2}q}
-fraction of the affine lines
L\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
, there is a set of points
A_{L}\subseteq L
of size
\mu(A_{L})\geq\varepsilon-\delta
, such that the univariate functions
f_{1}|_{L},\ldots f_{k}|_{L}
have correlated agreement with degree
d
on
A_{L}
. That is, for each
i\in[k]
, there is a univariate degree
d
function
h_{i}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfying

f_{i}|_{A_{L}}=h_{i}|_{A_{L}}.

Proof.

Fix
\delta\geq 1/\sqrt{q}
. For each choice of
(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\sim{\sf prox}(k,d,q,q^{\prime})
according to the proximity generator, let
B_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}
be the set of points on which
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{i}\cdot f_{i}
and its closest degree
d
function agree. Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{k}
be the set of tuples
(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})
such that
\mu(B_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})\geq\varepsilon
. By assumption,

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\in S]\geq 2k{\sf err}(d,q,q^{\prime}).

Fix a
(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\in S
and let

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\left\{L\in\mathcal{L}\;|\;|L\cap B_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}|\leq(\varepsilon-\delta)|L|\right\}

be the set of lines that have small intersection with
\mathcal{B}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}
. Applying
Lemma

2.4
with the lines versus points graph, we have

\delta\leq\left|\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime},x\in L}[x\in B_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}]-\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}}[x\in B_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}]\right|\leq\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\mu_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}}.

Thus,
\mu_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{q\cdot\delta^{2}}
, and so

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in S}\left[\Pr_{L}\left[{\sf agr}_{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{i}\cdot f_{i}|_{L}\right)\geq\varepsilon-\delta\right]\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{q\cdot\delta^{2}}.

Switching the order of expectations above, we get,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{L}\left[\Pr_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in S}\left[{\sf agr}_{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{i}\cdot f_{i}|_{L}\right)\geq\varepsilon-\delta\right]\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{\delta^{2}q}.

By an averaging argument, it follows that for at least
1-2/(\delta^{2}q)
fraction of affine lines
L
, we have,

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in S}\left[{\sf agr}_{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{i}\cdot f_{i}|_{L}\right)\geq\varepsilon-\delta\right]\geq 0.5.

Call these affine lines good. It remains to show that
f_{1}|_{L},\ldots,f_{k}|_{L}
have correlated agreement for every good
L
.

To this end, note that
Theorem

4.1
can be applied. Indeed,
0.5
-fraction of
S
is at least
k\cdot{\sf err}(d,q,q^{\prime})
fraction of the proximity generators sampled from
{\sf prox}(k,d,q,q^{\prime})
, so it follows that for every good line
L
, the univariate functions
f_{1}|_{L},\ldots,f_{k}|_{L}
satisfy the assumption in
Theorem

4.1
:

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}\left[{\sf agr}_{d}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{i}\cdot f_{i}|_{L}\right)\geq\varepsilon\right]\geq k\cdot{\sf err}(d,q,q^{\prime}).

Therefore, by
Theorem

4.1
for every good line
L
, there is a set of points
A_{L}
of size
\mu(A_{L})\geq\varepsilon-\delta
on which
f_{1}|_{L},\ldots,f_{k}|_{L}
each agree with some degree
d
function.
∎

C.1
Proof of
Theorem

4.4

Proof of
Theorem

4.4
.

Suppose the setting of
Theorem

4.4
. We apply
Claim

C.1
with
\delta=\frac{100}{\sqrt{q}}
, and let
\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}
be the set of lines given. Thus, for each
L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}
, the functions
f_{1}|_{L},\ldots,f_{k}|_{L}
have
\varepsilon-\delta
correlated agreement with degree
d
, and we let
A_{L}\subseteq L
be the site of this correlated agreement.
For each
f_{i}
, define a lines table
T_{i}
using the degree
d
functions that are correlated with
f_{i}
on the good lines. Specifically, for each
i\in[k]
, and each good line
L
, let
T_{i}[L]
be the degree
d
function such that

T_{i}[L]|_{A_{L}}=f_{i}|_{A_{L}}.

For every line
L\notin\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}
, set
T_{i}[L]
to be a randomly chosen degree
d
function. Now note that
T_{i}
and
f_{i}
pass the line versus point test with probability at least
0.999\varepsilon
. Indeed, for each
i\in[k]

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T_{i}[L](x)=f_{i}(x)]\geq\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L}}[L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}]\cdot\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}},x\in L}[T_{i}[L](x)=f_{i}(x)]\geq\left(1-\frac{2}{\delta^{2}q}\right)(\varepsilon-\delta),

(30)

and this final quantity is at least
\frac{9998}{10000}(\varepsilon-\delta)\geq 0.999\varepsilon
. Applying
Theorem

3.9
, it follows that
f_{i}
has agreement at least

0.999\varepsilon-\left(\frac{d}{q}\right)^{\tau_{{\sf line}}}\geq 0.998\varepsilon

with a degree
d
function. Moreover, this holds for every
i\in[k]
, so we are done.
∎

C.2
Proof of
Theorem

4.2

Proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Suppose the setting of
Theorem

4.2
and apply
Claim

C.1
. Call the lines satisfying
Claim

C.1
good, and denote the set of good lines by
\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}
. For each
f_{i}
, we will define a lines table,
T_{i}
, using the degree
d
functions that are correlated with
f_{i}
on the good lines. Specifically, for each
i\in[k]
, and each good line
L
, let
T_{i}[L]
be the degree
d
function agreeing with
f_{i}
on
A_{L}
.

For every line other line,
L
that is not good, set
T_{i}[L]
to be a randomly chosen degree
d
function. Then
T_{i}
and
f_{i}
pass the line versus point test with probability at least

0.98\cdot(\varepsilon-10/\sqrt{q})\geq 0.97\varepsilon.

Indeed, for each
i\in[k]

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T_{i}[L](x)=f_{i}(x)]\geq\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L}}[L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}]\cdot\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}\\
x\in L\end{subarray}}[T_{i}[L](x)=f_{i}(x)]\geq 0.998\cdot(\varepsilon-10/\sqrt{q})\geq 0.97\varepsilon.

(31)

Now applying
Theorem

A.16
, we get that for each
i\in[k]
, there is a list of
M\leq(5/(0.97k\varepsilon))^{7}
degree
d
functions
\{F_{i,1},\ldots F_{i,M}\}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
such that,

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[T_{i}[L](x)=f_{i}(x)\land T_{i}[L]\notin\{F_{i,j}|_{L}\}_{j=1}^{M}]\leq\frac{0.97\varepsilon}{10k}.

(32)

For each
i\in[k]
, let
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}=\{L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}\;|\;T_{i}[L]=F_{i,j}|_{L}\}
. We have

\displaystyle\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[L\in\cap_{i}(\cup_{j}\mathcal{L}_{i,j})]

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}\land x\in A_{L}]-\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L},x\in L}[L\in\mathcal{L}_{{\sf good}}\land x\in A_{L}\land(\cup_{i}T_{i}[L]\notin\{F_{i,j}|_{L}\}_{j=1}^{M})]

\displaystyle\qquad\geq 0.97\varepsilon-\frac{0.97\varepsilon}{10k}\cdot k

\displaystyle\qquad\geq 0.87\varepsilon,

where in the second transition we are using (
31
), (
32
). Since this holds for every
i
, we can find
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k})\in[M]^{k}
such that:

\Pr_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\left[L\in\bigcap_{i\in[k]}\mathcal{L}_{i,j_{i}}\right]\geq 0.87\varepsilon\cdot\frac{1}{M^{k}}\geq\frac{1}{100q^{1/2}},

where in the last transition we are using the assumption on the size of
\varepsilon
. Let
\mathcal{L}^{\star}=\bigcap_{i\in[k]}\mathcal{L}_{i,j_{i}}
. For each
i\in[k]
, we have that,
f_{i}
and the degree
d
function
F_{i,j_{i}}
agree on
A^{\star}=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{\star}}A_{L}
. To conclude, we use
Lemma

2.4
with the lines versus points graph to lower bound the size of
A^{\star}
:

\mu(A^{\star})\geq\varepsilon-\frac{10}{\sqrt{q}}-\frac{q^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{\mu_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\star})}}\geq\varepsilon-\frac{10}{\sqrt{q}}-\frac{1}{10q^{1/4}}\geq 0.999\varepsilon.\qed

C.3
Proof of
Theorem

4.3

Suppose the setting of
Theorem

4.3
. Our strategy will be to first show correlated agreement with a degree
2d
function, and then use list decoding and the Schwartz-Zippel lemma to show that this agreement must actually be with a degree
(d,d)
function.

To this end, let
\mathcal{H}
consist of all pairs of degree
2d
functions
(h^{(i)}_{1},h^{(i)}_{2})
, such that there exists a set
A_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
of size at least
0.99\varepsilon
satisfying
h^{(i)}_{j}|_{A_{i}}=f_{j}|_{A_{i}}
for all
j\in[2]
. By
Theorem

3.8
, we have
|\mathcal{H}|=M\leq\frac{2}{\varepsilon}
. Label the tuples in
\mathcal{H}
as
(h^{(i)}_{1},h^{(i)}_{2})
for
i\in[M]
. For each
i
, let
A_{i}
be the set on which
f_{1},f_{2}
have correlated agreement with
(h^{(i)}_{1},h^{(i)}_{2})
and let
A=\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}A_{i}
. If for any
i
we have that
h^{(i)}_{1}
and
h^{(i)}_{2}
are both degree
(d,d)
functions, then we are done, so suppose this is not the case.
Choosing
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, define the following events:

•

E_{1}
is the event that there exists a degree
(d,d)
function
H
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
satisfying
\mu(V)\geq\varepsilon
,
\mu(V\cap A)\leq M\cdot\frac{2d}{q}
and
\left(\xi_{1}\cdot f_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot f_{2}\right)|_{V}=H|_{V}
.

•

E_{2}
is the event that there exists a degree
(d,d)
function
H
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
satisfying
\mu(V)\geq\varepsilon
,
\mu(V\cap A)>M\cdot\frac{2d}{q}
and
\left(\xi_{1}\cdot f_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot f_{2}\right)|_{V}=H|_{V}
.

Note that

\Pr[E_{1}]+\Pr[E_{2}]\geq\Pr[{\sf agr}_{(d,d)}(\xi_{1}\cdot f_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot f_{2})\geq\varepsilon]\geq 2\cdot{\sf err}(2d,q,q^{\prime}),

(33)

where the second inequality is the assumption from
Theorem

4.3
. We first bound
\Pr[E_{1}]
.

Claim C.2
.

We have
\Pr[E_{1}]<{\sf err}(2d,q,q^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that the inequality above is false. For each
j\in[2]
, define
f^{\prime}_{j}
by setting
f^{\prime}_{j}(x)=f_{j}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}\setminus A
and setting
f^{\prime}_{j}(x)=x_{1}^{d+1}x_{2}^{d}
for all
x\in A
. Then by our assumption, we have

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}\left[{\sf agr}_{2d}\left(\xi_{1}\cdot f^{\prime}_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot f^{\prime}_{2}\right)\geq 0.999\varepsilon\right]\geq\Pr_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[E_{1}]\geq{\sf err}(2d,q,q^{\prime}).

(34)

Indeed, if
\xi_{1}\cdot f_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot f_{2}
agrees with some degree
(d,d)
function on
V
, then
\xi_{1}\cdot f^{\prime}_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot f^{\prime}_{2}
agrees with the same function on
V\setminus A
. Thus, if
E_{1}
occurs, then there is such
V
satisfying

\mu(V\setminus A)\geq\mu(V)-\mu(V\cap A)\geq\varepsilon-M\cdot\frac{2d}{q}>0.999\varepsilon.

Applying
Theorem

4.2
to
Equation

34
, we get that there exists a set
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
of size at least
0.998\varepsilon
and a pair of degree
2d
functions
(g_{1},g_{2})
such that
g_{j}|_{U}=f^{\prime}_{j}|_{U}
for all
j\in[2]
.

However, we claim that this is a contradiction because our construction of
f^{\prime}_{1}
and
f^{\prime}_{2}
guarantees that they cannot have such high correlated agreement with degree
2d
. First, they cannot have significant correlated agreement outside of
A
. Indeed, for any pair of degree
2d
functions
(g_{1},g_{2})
, we have,

\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}\setminus A}[g_{1}(x)=f^{\prime}_{1}(x)\land g_{2}(x)=f^{\prime}_{2}(x)]=\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}\setminus A}[g_{1}(x)=f_{1}(x)\land g_{2}(x)=f_{2}(x)]\leq 0.99\varepsilon.

The equality is because
f^{\prime}_{j}
and
f_{j}
agree outside of
A
for
j\in[2]
and the inequality is by the definition of
\mathcal{H}
and
A
. On the other hand,
f^{\prime}_{1}
and
f^{\prime}_{2}
cannot have significant correlated agreement inside of
A
either as

\Pr_{x\in A}[g_{1}(x)=f^{\prime}_{1}(x)\land g_{2}(x)=f^{\prime}_{2}(x)]\leq\frac{2d+1}{q}

by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma. Combining these two inequalities, we get that any set
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
where
f^{\prime}_{1}
and
f^{\prime}_{2}
have correlated agreement with degree
2d
can have size at most

\mu(U)\leq 0.99\varepsilon+\frac{2d+1}{q}<0.998\varepsilon,

contradicting our prior conclusion.
∎

Claim

C.2
and (
33
) give that
\Pr[E_{2}]\geq{\sf err}(2d,q,q^{\prime})
. From here, we can conclude the proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

Recall that we assume every
(h^{(i)}_{1},h^{(2)}_{2})\in\mathcal{H}
is a pair of functions whose individual degree is not
(d,d)
. Thus, for each
(h^{(i)}_{1},h^{(2)}_{2})\in\mathcal{H}
, we have that
\xi_{1}\cdot h^{(i)}_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot h^{(i)}_{2}
is degree
(d,d)
with probability at most
1/q^{\prime}
when
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
are chosen uniformly at random. Letting
E_{3}
denote the event that this is the case for some
i\in[M]
, we get that
\Pr[E_{3}]\leq M/q^{\prime}
. Thus, we get that

\Pr_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[E_{2}\land\overline{E_{3}}]>{\sf err}(2d,q,q^{\prime})-\frac{M}{q^{\prime}}>0,

so there exist coefficients
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
such that the following holds for
F=\xi_{1}\cdot f_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot f_{2}
:

•

There exists a degree
(d,d)
function
H
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}
such that
\mu(V\cap A)>M\cdot\frac{2d}{q}
and
F|_{V}=H|_{V}
.

•

The function
\xi_{1}\cdot h^{(i)}_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot h^{(i)}_{2}
is not degree
(d,d)
for any
i\in[M]
. In particular,
\xi_{1}\cdot h^{(i)}_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot h^{(i)}_{2}\neq H
.

We now reach a contradiction. First, by
a union bound
\mu(V\cap A)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mu(V\cap A_{i})
.
For each
i\in[M]
and
x\in V\cap A_{i}
we have

\xi_{1}\cdot h^{(i)}_{1}(x)+\xi_{2}\cdot h^{(i)}_{2}(x)=\xi_{1}\cdot f_{1}(x)+\xi_{2}\cdot f_{2}(x)=F(x)=H(x),

where the first equality is because
x\in A_{i}
, the second equality is by definition, and the third equality is because
x\in V
. As a result,

\mu(V\cap A_{i})\leq\Pr_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}}[\xi_{1}\cdot h^{(i)}_{1}(x)+\xi_{2}\cdot h^{(i)}_{2}(x)=H(x)]\leq\frac{2d}{q}

by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma and the fact that
\xi_{1}\cdot h^{(i)}_{1}+\xi_{2}\cdot h^{(i)}_{2}\neq H
are distinct functions with total degree at most
2d
. It follows that
\mu(V\cap A)\leq M\cdot\frac{2d}{q}
, and contradiction.
∎

Appendix D
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(2\sqrt{d},f,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
Construction: Proof of
Lemma

8.1

We construct
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,2\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
according to
Lemma

8.1
here. As the IOPP is fairly simple, we give the construction of
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,2\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
directly, instead of constructing a Poly-IOP first and then compiling it.

IOP 9

Reed-Solomon IOP:
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,2\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})

1:

P:
The prover sends
f_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
. We will assume that
f_{1}(0)=f(0)
. In order for this assumption to work, the verifier queries
f(0)
to obtain the value of
f_{1}(0)
.

2:

Define the function
f_{2}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
as follows:

f_{2}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{f(x)-f_{1}(x)}{x^{\sqrt{d}}}\text{ if }x\neq 0,\\
0\text{ if }x=0.\end{cases}

In the honest case,
f_{2}
has degree at most
\sqrt{d}
and,

f(x)=f_{1}(x)+x^{\sqrt{d}}f_{2}(x),\;\forall x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}.

3:

V:
The verifier chooses
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random and sets
g:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
to be

g(x)=f_{1}(x)+\gamma\cdot f_{2}(x).

4:

P+V:
Both parties run
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(g,\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.1
.

The length, round complexity, and query complexities are straightforward to verify. For the completeness, if
f\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[2\sqrt{d},\mathbb{F}_{q}]
, then its low degree extension
\widehat{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq 2\sqrt{d}}[x]
can be decomposed as
\widehat{f}=\widehat{f}_{1}+x^{\sqrt{d}}\widehat{f}_{2}

for
\widehat{f}_{1},\widehat{f}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq\sqrt{d}}[x]
.
The honest prover sends
f_{1}
to be the evaluation of
\widehat{f}_{1}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. In this case, one can check that
f_{2}
as defined in step 2 is the evaluation of
\widehat{f}_{2}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Thus, both
f_{1},f_{2}\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[\sqrt{d},\mathbb{F}_{q}]
. It follows that
g\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[\sqrt{d},\mathbb{F}_{q}]
with probability
1
and the remainder of the completeness follows from that of
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(f,\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
.

For the soundness case, suppose that

{\sf agr}(f,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[2\sqrt{d},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\leq\varepsilon_{k-1}.

(35)

Note that

\Pr_{\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}}[{\sf agr}(g,\RS_{q^{\prime}}[\sqrt{d},\mathbb{F}_{q}])\geq\varepsilon_{k-1}]\leq\frac{{\sf poly}(\sqrt{d},q)}{q^{\prime}}\leq 2^{-\lambda}.

(36)

Indeed, otherwise by
Lemma

4.7

f_{1}
and
f_{2}
have
\varepsilon_{k-1}
correlated agreement with degree
\sqrt{d}
functions
F_{1},F_{2}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
, implying
{\sf agr}(f,F_{1}+x^{\sqrt{d}}\cdot F_{2})\geq\varepsilon_{k-1}
.
Since
F_{1}+x^{\sqrt{d}}\cdot F_{2}\in\RS_{q^{\prime}}[2\sqrt{d},\mathbb{F}_{q}]
, this contradicts (
35
).

With (
35
) established, the round-by-round soundness then follows from the round-by-round soundness of
\texttt{RS-IOPP}(g,\sqrt{d},q,\lambda,\varepsilon_{k-1})
.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs for
Section

10

E.1
Univariate Sumcheck

Towards the proof of
Lemma

10.2
, we will first describe a univariate sumcheck Poly-IOP, and then use this in the multivariate sumcheck Poly-IOP. The following fact will be useful.

Fact E.1
.

If
H
is a multiplicative subgroup of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
and
\widehat{R}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq|H|-1}[x]
be a polynomial, then

\sum_{\alpha\in H}\widehat{R}(\alpha)=\widehat{R}(0)\cdot|H|.

Proof.

Writing
H=\{w^{0},\ldots,w^{|H|-1}\}
for some
w
,
for every
1\leq j\leq|H|-1
we have that

\sum\limits_{w\in H}w^{j}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{|H|-1}(w^{j})^{i}=(w^{j}-1)^{-1}((w^{j})^{|H|}-1)=0

via the geometric sum formula. Expanding
R
into a linear combination of monomials and using this fact gives the result.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

There is a Poly-IOP with the following guarantees:

•

Input: A Polynomial
\widehat{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d^{\prime}}[x]
, a multiplicative subgroup
H\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
of size
d+1
, and a target sum
\alpha
.

•

Completeness: If
\sum_{b\in H}\widehat{f}(b)=\alpha
then the honest prover makes the verifier accept with probability
1
.

•

Round-by-Round Soundness:
\frac{d^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}
.

•

Initial State: The initial state is doomed if and only if
\sum_{b\in H}\widehat{f}(b)\neq\alpha
.

•

Number of Polynomials:
O(1)
.

•

Round Complexity:
O(1)
.

•

Input Query Complexity:
\widehat{f}
is queried
1
time.

•

Proof Query Complexity:
O(1)
.

Proof.

We begin with a formal description of the Poly-IOP:

IOP 10

Univariate Sumcheck Poly-IOP

1:

P:
The prover sends
\widehat{F}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d^{\prime}-|H|}[x]
, and
\widehat{R}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq|H|-2}[x]
. In the honest case,

\widehat{f}=\frac{\alpha}{|H|}+\widehat{V}_{H}\cdot\widehat{F}+x\cdot\widehat{R}.

2:

V:
The verifier chooses
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random and checks if

\widehat{f}(\gamma)=\frac{\alpha}{|H|}+\widehat{V}_{H}(\gamma)\cdot\widehat{F}(\gamma)+\gamma\cdot\widehat{R}(\gamma).

If the check fails then the verifier rejects.

The number of polynomials, round complexity, input query complexity, and proof query complexity are clear.

Completeness:

assume that
\sum_{b\in H}\widehat{f}(b)=\alpha
. By
Lemma

3.14
we may write

\widehat{f}=\widehat{V}_{H}\cdot\widehat{F}+\widehat{R^{\prime}},

for
\widehat{F}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d^{\prime}-|H|}[x]
, and
\widehat{R}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq|H|-1}[x]
that agrees with
\widehat{f}
on
H
. Furthermore, by
Fact

E.1
, it must be the case that
\widehat{R}(0)=\alpha/|H|
. Therefore, the honest prover can find
\widehat{F}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d^{\prime}-|H|}[x]
and
\widehat{R}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq|H|-2}[x]
which satisfy

\widehat{f}=\alpha/|H|+\widehat{V}_{H}\cdot\widehat{F}+x\widehat{R}.

It follows that the equation in step 2 is satisfied for every
\gamma
and the verifier always accepts.

Round-by-round soundness:

there is only one round of interaction, after which we define the state as doomed if and only if the check fails and the verifier rejects. If the initial state is doomed, then it follows that for any
\widehat{F}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq d^{\prime}-|H|}[x]
and
\widehat{R}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq|H|-2}[x]
,

\widehat{f}\neq\alpha/|H|+\widehat{V}_{H}\cdot\widehat{F}+x\widehat{R}.

This is because the right hand side is a polynomial summing to
\alpha
over
H
by
Fact

E.1
. As the polynomial on the right hand side has degree
d^{\prime}
, by the Schwartz-Zippel lemma, the check in step
2
fails with probability at least
1-d^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.
∎

E.1.1
Proof of
Lemma

10.2

The IOP is described in
IOP

11
.

IOP 11

Multivariate Sumcheck Poly-IOP

1:

P:
The prover sends
\widehat{F}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}^{\leq 6d}_{q^{\prime}}[x]
. In the honest case,

\widehat{F}_{1}(x)=\sum_{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(x,\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}),

and

\sum_{\gamma\in H}\widehat{F}_{1}(\gamma)=b.

(37)

2:

V:
The verifier chooses
\zeta_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random and sends it to the prover. The verifier queries
\widehat{F}_{1}(\zeta_{1})
.

3:

P:
The prover sends
\widehat{F}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq 6d}[x]
. In the honest case,

\widehat{F}_{2}(x)=\sum_{\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},x,\gamma_{3}),

and

\sum_{\gamma\in H}\widehat{F}_{2}(\gamma)=\widehat{F}_{1}(\zeta_{1}).

(38)

4:

V:
The verifier chooses
\zeta_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}
uniformly at random and queries
\widehat{F}_{2}(\zeta_{2})
.

5:

P + V:
Both parties run the univariate sumcheck protocol to check (
37
), (
38
), and

\sum_{\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\gamma_{3})=\widehat{F}_{2}(\zeta_{2}),

(39)

hold.

6:

V:
The verifier accepts if they reach this stage. That is, they accept if they have not rejected yet during steps 2,5, or 7.

The number of polynomials, round complexity, input query complexity, and proof query complexity are clear. We discuss the completeness and Round-by-Round soundness below.

Completeness.

In the completeness case, the prover sends
\widehat{F}_{1}(x)=\sum_{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(x,\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
. Note that indeed
\widehat{F}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq 6d}[x,y]
and that (
37
) is satisfied. As a result, the univariate sumcheck in step 2 passes with probability
1
by the completeness in
Lemma

E.2
. In step
4
, the prover sends
\widehat{F}_{2}=\sum_{\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},x,\gamma_{3})
. Once again
\widehat{F}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq 6d}[x]
and Equation (
38
) is satisfied, so step 5 always passes by the completeness in
Lemma

E.2
. We also have
\sum_{\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\gamma_{3})=\widehat{F}_{2}(\zeta_{2})
in this case, so the sumcheck in step 7 is once passes by the completeness in
Lemma

E.2
and the verifier accepts at the end.

Round-by-Round Soundness.

After the first round of interaction (step 2), the state function is as follows.

State Function 13
.

The state is doomed if and only if at least one of the following holds

•

\widehat{F}_{1}(\zeta_{1})\neq\sum_{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
.

•

\sum_{\gamma\in H}\widehat{F}_{1}(\gamma)\neq b
.

After the second round of interaction (step 4), the state function is as follows.

State Function 14
.

The state is doomed if and only if at least one of the following holds

•

\widehat{F}_{2}(\zeta_{2})\neq\sum_{\gamma_{3}}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\gamma_{3})
.

•

\sum_{\gamma\in H}\widehat{F}_{2}(\gamma)\neq\widehat{F}_{1}(\zeta_{1})
.

•

\sum_{\gamma\in H}\widehat{F}_{1}(\gamma)\neq b
.

Proof.

Suppose the initial state is doomed, meaning
\sum_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\neq b
. If the prover sends
\widehat{F}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}^{\leq 6d}[x]
not satisfying (
37
), then
State Function

13
is doomed, and soundness of the first round is done. Otherwise, (
37
) is satisfied and it must be the case that

\widehat{F}_{1}(x)\neq\sum_{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(x,\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}).

Note that both sides of the above equation are degree at most
6d
polynomials in
x
. By the Schwartz-Zippel lemma, we have

\widehat{F}_{1}(\zeta_{1})\neq\sum_{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})

with probability at least
1-6d/q^{\prime}\geq 1-2^{-\lambda}
. This shows soundness for the first round of interaction.

For the second round of interaction, suppose that
State Function

13
is doomed. If it is due to the second item in
State Function

13
, then
State Function

14
is automatically doomed by item 3 of
State Function

14
. If the prover sends
\widehat{F}_{2}
not satisfying (
38
), then
State Function

14
is automatically doomed by item 2 of
State Function

14
. Otherwise, it must be the case that

\widehat{F}_{2}(x)\neq\sum_{\gamma_{3}\in H}\widehat{f}(\zeta_{1},x,\gamma_{3}),

in which case item 1 of
State Function

14
holds with probability at least
1-6d/q^{\prime}\geq 2^{-\lambda}
. This establishes soundness of the second round of interaction.

For the remainder of the Round-by-Round soundness, note that going into step 5, the state is doomed if and only if at least one of (
37
), (
38
), or (
39
) is not satisfied. In this case the Round-by-Round soundness of
Lemma

E.2
takes care of the soundness of the remaining rounds.
∎
